### Historical Audit Limitations

- Git history records committed changes, not every edit.
- Reflog records local Git reference movements, not every action.
- File timestamps are evidence, not proof of when or how a change occurred.
- Deleted files may be visible in Git history but absent from the current inventory.
- Actions that left no surviving evidence cannot be reconstructed.

## File Modified

- **Detected:** 2026-11-25T17:45:48+01:00
- **Path:** `workflow.md`
- **Size:** 47,171 bytes
- **SHA-256:** `00337ba84008c33816762dda59bfbdf90b08928bb3754faa9a0e13f83ef1679d`

### Text Change

```diff
--- previous
+++ current
@@ -91,15 +91,83 @@
 
 ### 4.2 Mandatory ICP Qualification
 
-| Condition                | PASS                                                                                                                                       | FAIL                                                                     | Insufficient evidence |
-| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------- |
-| Nigerian presence        | Current Nigerian business operations supported by evidence.                                                                                | No Nigerian operations established or operations definitively ceased.    | `UNKNOWN`             |
-| Business identity        | Operating entity distinguishable through credible sources.                                                                                 | Candidate is the wrong entity or identity is disproved.                  | `UNKNOWN`             |
-| Segment fit              | Business activity matches one approved segment.                                                                                            | Business is outside both approved segments.                              | `UNKNOWN`             |
-| Active operations        | Current commercial activity supported.                                                                                                     | Business is dormant or relevant operations have ceased.                  | `UNKNOWN`             |
-| Physical operating model | Physical retail outlets or genuine wholesale/distribution operations supported.                                                            | Exclusively online retail or no qualifying physical business operations. | `UNKNOWN`             |
-| Payments relevance       | Meaningful recurring business collections, settlements or supplier-payment requirements are plausible and supported by the business model. | No meaningful relevance to the proposition is established.               | `UNKNOWN`             |
-
+Every account must be assessed against all six conditions. Each condition must receive exactly one outcome: PASS, FAIL or UNKNOWN.
+
+An account is QUALIFIED only when all six conditions are PASS.
+
+An account is DISQUALIFIED when at least one condition is definitively FAIL.
+
+An account is FURTHER_RESEARCH when no condition is FAIL but at least one condition is UNKNOWN.
+
+Missing evidence must never be treated as evidence of failure.
+
+Condition
+
+UNKNOWN criteria
+
+Nigerian presence
+
+Nigerian presence cannot be established or reliably ruled out
+
+Business identity
+
+Available information is insufficient to establish the correct business identity
+
+Segment fit
+
+Available evidence does not establish which approved segment, if any, applies
+
+Active operations
+
+Available evidence is stale, contradictory or insufficient to establish current operations
+
+Physical operating model
+
+The physical operating model cannot be established
+
+Payments relevance
+
+The nature or significance of its payment activity cannot be established
+
+Evidence requirements
+
+Every PASS or FAIL must have supporting evidence recorded.
+
+Evidence should include:
+
+The source name.
+
+The direct source URL.
+
+The specific fact being established.
+
+The date accessed or verified.
+
+A short explanation of why the evidence supports the decision.
+
+Prefer company-owned sources and credible independent sources. A search-result snippet alone is not sufficient evidence where the underlying source can be accessed.
+
+For active operations, use evidence that supports current trading or operating activity. An old company profile or undated directory entry does not independently establish that a business is still operating.
+
+For physical operating model, record actual evidence of outlets, warehouses, depots or other relevant facilities. Do not infer a physical footprint merely from the company's industry classification.
+
+For payments relevance, assess the business model and its transaction activities. Do not treat the existence of payment activity as proof of payment friction, poor banking service, reconciliation problems or purchase intent.
+
+Qualification boundaries
+
+Five or more physical locations is a prioritisation heuristic, not a mandatory ICP qualification condition.
+
+A single-location supermarket can qualify if it passes all six conditions.
+
+An FMCG distributor does not need to operate retail outlets.
+
+A distributor's warehouse or depot can establish a physical operating model without being classified as a supermarket branch.
+
+High transaction volume, payment complexity and consolidated payment-management requirements must be evidenced before being described as established characteristics of an account.
+
+Finance recruitment is a signal to investigate, not a mandatory ICP qualification condition.
+
+A detected signal cannot override an ICP failure.
 **ICP decision rules:**
 
 - `QUALIFIED`: All six mandatory conditions are `PASS`.
```

## File Modified

- **Detected:** 2026-11-25T17:48:08+01:00
- **Path:** `workflow.md`
- **Size:** 48,361 bytes
- **SHA-256:** `ca307935fa5d2428351d6025ddd2c38e93cc7e5a3d6e03683c13d1d12ca87e75`

### Text Change

```diff
--- previous
+++ current
@@ -98,6 +98,68 @@
 
 
 
+### Mandatory ICP Qualification
+
+Every account must be assessed against all six conditions. Each condition must receive exactly one outcome: `PASS`, `FAIL` or `UNKNOWN`.
+
+An account is `QUALIFIED` only when all six conditions are `PASS`.
+
+An account is `DISQUALIFIED` when at least one condition is definitively `FAIL`.
+
+An account is `FURTHER_RESEARCH` when no condition is `FAIL` but at least one condition is `UNKNOWN`.
+
+Missing evidence must never be treated as evidence of failure.
+
+| Condition                | UNKNOWN criteria                                                                           |
+| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
+| Nigerian presence        | Nigerian presence cannot be established or reliably ruled out                              |
+| Business identity        | Available information is insufficient to establish the correct business identity           |
+| Segment fit              | Available evidence does not establish which approved segment, if any, applies              |
+| Active operations        | Available evidence is stale, contradictory or insufficient to establish current operations |
+| Physical operating model | The physical operating model cannot be established                                         |
+| Payments relevance       | The nature or significance of its payment activity cannot be established                   |
+
+#### Evidence requirements
+
+Every `PASS` or `FAIL` must have supporting evidence recorded.
+
+Evidence should include:
+
+- The source name.
+- The direct source URL.
+- The specific fact being established.
+- The date accessed or verified.
+- A short explanation of why the evidence supports the decision.
+
+Prefer company-owned sources and credible independent sources. A search-result snippet alone is not sufficient evidence where the underlying source can be accessed.
+
+For active operations, use evidence that supports current trading or operating activity. An old company profile or undated directory entry does not independently establish that a business is still operating.
+
+For physical operating model, record actual evidence of outlets, warehouses, depots or other relevant facilities. Do not infer a physical footprint merely from the company's industry classification.
+
+For payments relevance, assess the business model and its transaction activities. Do not treat the existence of payment activity as proof of payment friction, poor banking service, reconciliation problems or purchase intent.
+
+#### Qualification boundaries
+
+- Five or more physical locations is a prioritisation heuristic, not a mandatory ICP qualification condition.
+- A single-location supermarket can qualify if it passes all six conditions.
+- An FMCG distributor does not need to operate retail outlets.
+- A distributor's warehouse or depot can establish a physical operating model without being classified as a supermarket branch.
+- High transaction volume, payment complexity and consolidated payment-management requirements must be evidenced before being described as established characteristics of an account.
+- Finance recruitment is a signal to investigate, not a mandatory ICP qualification condition.
+- A detected signal cannot override an ICP failure.
+
+#### Fit tier
+
+Assign a fit tier only after ICP qualification.
+
+`EXCELLENT_FIT`: Strong evidence of substantial operating scale, such as five or more physical locations or a significant distribution footprint, alongside credible high-frequency transaction activity and relevant financial operations complexity.
+
+`REASONABLE_FIT`: A qualified business with a credible payment-management use case, including a single-location high-volume retailer or an FMCG distributor with meaningful B2B collection or supplier-payment activity.
+
+`UNASSESSED`: Evidence is insufficient to determine relative commercial attractiveness.
+
+The fit tier is a prioritisation assessment. It must not change the mandatory ICP outcome.
 
 **ICP decision rules:**
 
```

### Text Change

--- previous

@@ -160,24 +160,7 @@
 - Missing information is not a negative finding.
 - A detected buying signal cannot override an ICP failure.
 
-### 4.3 Fit Tier and Prioritisation
-
-The ICP distinguishes stronger-fit accounts from reasonable-fit accounts. These tiers guide prioritisation; they do not replace the mandatory qualification decision.
-
-| Fit tier         | Qualification profile                                                                                                                           |
-| ---------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
-| `EXCELLENT_FIT`  | Businesses with five or more physical locations, high-frequency transaction activity and a relevant finance or treasury recruitment profile.    |
-| `REASONABLE_FIT` | Single-location, high-volume retailers, including large supermarkets, or distributors with a credible need for consolidated payment management. |
-| `UNASSESSED`     | Qualified account whose available evidence is not sufficient to assign a fit tier.                                                              |
-
-**Interpretation rules:**
-
-- Five or more locations is an initial prioritisation heuristic, not a mandatory ICP threshold.
-- A single-location business is not automatically disqualified if it otherwise meets the approved segment definition and mandatory conditions.
-- A distributor does not need to operate retail outlets. Its wholesale and distribution model must be verified.
-- High transaction volume and collection complexity should be evidenced where possible, not assumed from company size.
-- Finance recruitment is also a buying signal. Its presence may contribute to an `EXCELLENT_FIT` assessment but must be recorded separately as signal evidence.
-- Businesses fully dependent on B2B e-invoicing with no meaningful in-person collection or other relevant payment-management requirements are poor fit. Verify the actual business model before disqualification.
+
 
 ## 5. Signal Definitions and Detection

### Text Change

```diff
--- previous
+++ current
@@ -216,7 +216,126 @@
 
 The 180-day and 12-month windows are initial operating assumptions. Validate them during implementation.
 
-
+### Clay Signal Research Implementation Contract
+
+Both active signals are researched independently for every ICP-qualified account.
+
+The research process must identify an observable event, retain its source evidence and distinguish a verified event from a search result that merely appears relevant.
+
+The implementation must not assume that a particular Clay provider, search integration or enrichment source is available on the user's plan. Each proposed research method must first be tested in the actual Clay workspace.
+
+#### Signal 1: FINANCE_RECRUITMENT
+
+**Research objective:** Identify recent, attributable recruitment activity for finance, treasury or financial operations roles at the target business or a verified subsidiary.
+
+**Eligible roles include:**
+- Treasury Manager
+- Finance Manager
+- Financial Controller
+- Finance Operations Manager
+- Finance Analyst
+- Relevant accounting, treasury or financial control roles
+
+**Candidate source categories:**
+- The company's careers or recruitment page.
+- The company's verified LinkedIn presence or recruitment announcements.
+- Credible job boards and recruitment platforms.
+- Credible business or professional publications reporting the vacancy.
+
+**Candidate Clay implementation methods:**
+1. Test whether the available Clay search or research functionality can discover relevant vacancies using the company name and domain.
+2. Test whether a web-research AI column can return the vacancy URL, role, employer attribution, publication date and vacancy status.
+3. Where a candidate vacancy is already known, use its direct URL as the research input rather than relying on an AI-generated summary.
+4. If the available Clay functionality cannot retrieve adequate evidence, record the limitation and use the approved external research process. Do not fabricate a completed search.
+
+**Required evidence fields:**
+- `signal_type`
+- `signal_event_id`
+- `employer_name`
+- `employer_domain`
+- `role_title`
+- `role_responsibilities`
+- `source_name`
+- `source_url`
+- `publication_date`
+- `job_status`
+- `verification_date`
+- `evidence_excerpt`
+- `evidence_status`
+- `verification_notes`
+
+A vacancy qualifies only when the employer attribution, role relevance, publication date and vacancy status can be established sufficiently to support the decision.
+
+The active recency window is 180 days before the verification date.
+
+A role title alone is insufficient. The responsibilities must be relevant to finance, treasury, accounting, financial control or financial operations.
+
+#### Signal 2: MULTI_BRANCH_EXPANSION
+
+**Research objective:** Identify recent, attributable evidence that a qualified business is opening additional physical outlets, expanding its distribution footprint or entering additional geographic markets.
+
+**Eligible events include:**
+- A supermarket opening a new physical outlet.
+- A supermarket announcing a specific new outlet.
+- A business expanding into another city or state through physical operations.
+- An FMCG distributor opening a new depot or warehouse as part of genuine footprint expansion.
+- An FMCG distributor entering an additional geographic distribution market.
+
+**Candidate source categories:**
+- Company websites and official announcements.
+- Verified company social-media channels.
+- Credible business publications.
+- Credible local or industry publications reporting a specific opening or expansion.
+
+**Candidate Clay implementation methods:**
+1. Test whether the available Clay search or research functionality can discover expansion announcements using the company name and domain.
+2. Test whether a web-research AI column can return the specific event, location, source URL, publication date and event status.
+3. Where a candidate announcement is already known, use the direct source URL as the research input.
+4. If the available Clay functionality cannot retrieve adequate evidence, record the limitation and use the approved external research process. Do not classify an incomplete search as `NOT_DETECTED`.
+
+**Required evidence fields:**
+- `signal_type`
+- `signal_event_id`
+- `company_name`
+- `source_name`
+- `source_url`
+- `event_description`
+- `publication_date`
+- `event_date_or_period`
+- `event_location`
+- `expansion_status`
+- `verification_date`
+- `evidence_excerpt`
+- `evidence_status`
+- `verification_notes`
+
+Expansion status must be one of:
+
+`COMPLETED`, `ANNOUNCED`, `PLANNED`, `UNVERIFIED`.
+
+The active recency window is 12 months before the verification date.
+
+A specific, attributable announcement within the active window can qualify as a detected signal, even if the opening has not yet occurred. Its status must remain `ANNOUNCED` or `PLANNED`, as applicable. It must never be described as an operational location until completion is established.
+
+General statements about growth, ambition or future plans without a specific attributable expansion event do not qualify.
+
+#### Search coverage and execution controls
+
+For each signal search, record:
+- Search start and completion timestamps.
+- Source categories checked.
+- Search method used.
+- Candidate events discovered.
+- Candidates assessed and their outcomes.
+- Sources that could not be accessed.
+- Search errors, limitations and unresolved gaps.
+- Whether the available evidence supports sufficient coverage.
+
+A fixed number of search results does not independently establish sufficient coverage.
+
+The implementation must be tested on a small number of qualified accounts before scaling. Confirm that it can retrieve direct source URLs, distinguish publication dates from event dates, identify relevant roles or expansion events, and preserve unresolved candidates.
+
+Clay provider availability, credit consumption and search coverage must be recorded during this test. No specific provider or enrichment capability should be treated as available until verified in the actual workspace.
 
 
 
```

@@ -445,25 +445,57 @@
 
 ### 6.2 Signal Combination and Routing Rules
 
-Apply these rules only to `QUALIFIED` accounts after both searches have terminal outcomes.
-
-| Finance outcome       | Expansion outcome     | Routing outcome          | Priority     | Required commercial action                                                                                                                                           |
-| --------------------- | --------------------- | ------------------------ | ------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
-| `DETECTED`            | `DETECTED`            | `COMBINED_SIGNALS`       | `HIGHEST`    | Retain both verified events. Use the segment-specific finance and expansion angles. Choose the lead discussion topic from the actual vacancy and expansion evidence. |
-| `DETECTED`            | `NOT_DETECTED`        | `FINANCE_RECRUITMENT`    | `HIGH`       | Use the finance recruitment angle for the segment. Do not imply expansion.                                                                                           |
-| `NOT_DETECTED`        | `DETECTED`            | `MULTI_BRANCH_EXPANSION` | `HIGH`       | Use the expansion angle for the segment. Do not imply finance recruitment.                                                                                           |
-| `NOT_DETECTED`        | `NOT_DETECTED`        | `ICP_MONITORING`         | `MONITORING` | Retain the qualified account for future monitoring. Do not create a signal-led outreach claim.                                                                       |
-| Any other combination | Any other combination | `FURTHER_RESEARCH`       | `RESEARCH`   | Resolve incomplete, failed, unverified or historical evidence before releasing the account for signal-led outreach.                                                  |
-
-**Additional routing rules:**
-
-- Any `UNKNOWN`, `UNVERIFIED` or `HISTORICAL` outcome results in `FURTHER_RESEARCH`, unless the other search combination is already covered by a higher-confidence detected result. In all cases, unresolved evidence must remain visible.
-- A verified signal must not be discarded because the other search is incomplete.
-- If one signal is `DETECTED` and the other is unresolved, preserve the detected event and its provisional commercial angle. Keep the overall route at `FURTHER_RESEARCH`.
-- Historical or unverified evidence never substitutes for `DETECTED`.
-- An account with no detected signals is not automatically disqualified.
-- ICP failures route to `ICP_DISQUALIFIED`, not to signal-led prospecting.
-- ICP uncertainty routes to `FURTHER_RESEARCH` before signal research begins.
+### Signal Combination and Routing Rules
+
+Routing is determined by the terminal outcomes of the two independent signal searches.
+
+Only four combinations qualify for a definitive signal-led route:
+
+| Finance recruitment | Multi-branch expansion | Combination | Route | Priority |
+|---|---|---|---|---|
+| DETECTED | DETECTED | `COMBINED_SIGNALS` | Combined signals | HIGHEST |
+| DETECTED | NOT_DETECTED | `FINANCE_RECRUITMENT` | Finance recruitment | HIGH |
+| NOT_DETECTED | DETECTED | `MULTI_BRANCH_EXPANSION` | Multi-branch expansion | HIGH |
+| NOT_DETECTED | NOT_DETECTED | `ICP_MONITORING` | ICP monitoring | MONITORING |
+
+Every other combination routes to:
+
+`FURTHER_RESEARCH`
+
+with priority:
+
+`RESEARCH`
+
+This includes any combination containing `UNKNOWN`, `UNVERIFIED` or `HISTORICAL`, regardless of whether the other signal is `DETECTED`.
+
+#### Preservation of detected evidence
+
+An unresolved search must not erase or downgrade a separately verified event.
+
+For example:
+
+- Finance recruitment: `DETECTED`
+- Multi-branch expansion: `UNKNOWN`
+
+The account routes to `FURTHER_RESEARCH`, but the verified finance recruitment event remains recorded and the provisional commercial angle remains available for human review.
+
+The same principle applies when expansion is detected but finance recruitment remains unresolved.
+
+A `NOT_DETECTED` outcome is permitted only when the corresponding search is `COMPLETED`, coverage is sufficient and no qualifying or unresolved candidate remains.
+
+`FAILED`, `INSUFFICIENT_COVERAGE`, unfinished searches and unresolved candidates must never be converted into `NOT_DETECTED`.
+
+#### Routing restrictions
+
+- Signal detection does not establish purchase intent.
+- Signal detection does not establish a payment problem.
+- Signal detection does not establish dissatisfaction with an existing bank or payment provider.
+- The commercial angle is a research hypothesis, not a claim about the account.
+- `ICP_DISQUALIFIED` accounts cannot enter signal-led routing.
+- `FURTHER_RESEARCH` accounts cannot enter the contact-ready workflow.
+- AI may recommend a route using the approved deterministic rules but cannot approve an account for CRM processing or outreach.
+
+The route must retain the two underlying signal outcomes, the relevant evidence records, the rationale and any outstanding research gaps.
 
 ### 6.3 Required Routing Output
 
```

+### Clay Workbook Implementation Boundary
+
+The primary Clay workbook is company/account-first because both selected signals belong to the company rather than an individual contact.
+
+The workbook must use the following logical structure:
+
+1. **Accounts:** Main table containing company identity, ICP qualification, fit tier, signal-search status, routing and human-review status.
+2. **Signal Evidence:** Supporting records containing individual signal candidates, verified events, rejected candidates and their source evidence. Multiple evidence records may belong to one account.
+3. **Contacts:** A subsequent contact-research stage linked to qualified accounts. Contact discovery must not determine whether the underlying company passes the ICP.
+
+The main Accounts table must not treat a company as a separate account merely because it has multiple branches, vacancies or signal events.
+
+Company identity and domain are the primary deduplication controls. Potential duplicates must be reviewed rather than silently merged where identity is uncertain.
+
+#### Automation and approval
+
+- Automatic execution must remain disabled during initial testing.
+- Prove each enrichment, formula and AI column on a small sample before scaling.
+- Run paid or credit-consuming enrichments only after the preceding qualification gates pass.
+- Preserve failed, unresolved and rejected evidence rather than silently overwriting it.
+- Record the cost and credit consumption of each paid step.
+- Keep human approval separate from AI classification.
+- Only a human-approved account can progress to `CRM_READY`.
+- `CRM_READY` means approved for CRM processing. It does not mean that a CRM sync or outreach action has occurred.
+
+#### Initial implementation acceptance
+
+Before scaling the workbook, demonstrate that:
+- A company can be discovered and deduplicated.
+- All six mandatory ICP conditions can be assessed independently.
+- A failed or unknown ICP condition cannot be overridden by a detected signal.
+- Both signal searches can produce separate statuses and outcomes.
+- Each detected event has a direct, attributable evidence source.
+- Unresolved searches cannot become `NOT_DETECTED`.
+- The four approved routing combinations produce the correct routes.
+- Every other signal combination produces `FURTHER_RESEARCH`.
+- AI output follows the defined fields and allowed values.
+- A human approval is required before `CRM_READY`.
+- Enrichment costs, failed lookups and missing data are visible in the build log.
 
 ### 8.2 Retry and Recalculation
 
```

-
+Component	Responsibility

+Account discovery	Identifies businesses within the two approved target segments.
+Company enrichment	Establishes identity, Nigerian operations, physical operating model and other mandatory ICP evidence.
+Signal detection	Independently investigates finance recruitment and multi-branch expansion after mandatory ICP qualification.

+Account prioritisation	Applies deterministic routing using both signal outcomes, evidence quality and commercial relevance.

+Human review	Validates evidence, qualification, routing and contact readiness before approval.
+Feedback	Improves future research and qualification criteria.

 Feedback improves future prospecting runs rather than restarting completed accounts. Further research returns an account to enrichment for reassessment.
 
 ## 3. Core System Components
-
-| Component              | Responsibility                                                                         |
-| ---------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
-| Context OS             | Maintains company context, ICP definitions, buying signals and operating instructions. |
-| Account discovery      | Identifies businesses within the target segments using public sources.                 |
-| Company enrichment     | Collects firmographic, operational and commercial evidence.                            |
-| Signal detection       | Identifies relevant developments such as recruitment and geographic expansion.         |
-| ICP qualification      | Evaluates commercial and operational fit.                                              |
-| Account prioritisation | Ranks qualified accounts using fit, evidence and signal relevance.                     |
-| Buyer identification   | Identifies relevant decision-makers and influencers.                                   |
-| Clay                   | Proposed environment for enrichment, structuring and prospect preparation.             |
-| Human review           | Validates prospect records before activation.                                          |
-| Feedback               | Improves future research and qualification criteria.                                   |
+| Component | Responsibility |
+|---|---|
+| Context OS | Maintains company context, ICP definitions, buying signals and operating instructions. |
+| Account discovery | Identifies businesses within the two approved target segments. |
+| Company enrichment | Establishes identity, Nigerian operations, physical operating model and other mandatory ICP evidence. |
+| Signal detection | Independently investigates finance recruitment and multi-branch expansion after mandatory ICP qualification. |
+| ICP qualification | Evaluates the six mandatory conditions using PASS, FAIL or UNKNOWN. |
+| Account prioritisation | Applies deterministic routing using both signal outcomes, evidence quality and commercial relevance. |
+| Buyer identification | Identifies relevant decision-makers and influencers after account qualification and routing. |
+| Clay | Supports account enrichment, signal research, contact preparation and workflow execution. |
+| Human review | Validates evidence, qualification, routing and contact readiness before approval. |
+| Feedback | Improves future research and qualification criteria. |
 
 ## 4. Qualification and Prioritisation Logic
 
```

## File Modified

- **Path:** `architecture.md`

### Text Change

```diff
--- previous
+++ current
@@ -106,6 +106,21 @@
 
 **Buying intent:** What observable evidence suggests a relevant business need or reason to engage?
 
+**ICP fit:** Whether the business satisfies the six mandatory qualification conditions defined in `workflow.md`.
+
+Each condition receives one of three outcomes:
+
+- PASS
+- FAIL
+- UNKNOWN
+
+Overall qualification:
+
+- `QUALIFIED`: All six conditions PASS.
+- `DISQUALIFIED`: At least one condition definitively FAILS.
+- `FURTHER_RESEARCH`: No condition FAILS, but at least one remains UNKNOWN.
+
+Missing evidence must not be treated as a negative finding.
 ICP fit considers:
 
 - Industry and business model
```

 Missing evidence must not be treated as a negative finding.
 
-ICP fit considers:
-
-- Industry and business model
-- Geographic and physical operating footprint
-- Transaction volume and frequency, where available
-- Payment collection and reconciliation complexity
-- Financial and operational requirements
-- Potential relevance of verified Moniepoint products
-
-Buying signals initially include:
-
-- Finance and treasury recruitment
-- Multi-branch or geographic expansion
-- Warehouse, logistics and operations recruitment
-
-These signals indicate potential relevance, not confirmed purchase intent. Their recency, reliability and commercial significance must be assessed.
+Buying signals are restricted to two approved categories:
+
+- `FINANCE_RECRUITMENT`: Relevant finance, treasury, accounting or financial operations recruitment.
+- `MULTI_BRANCH_EXPANSION`: Verified or attributable expansion of physical retail or distribution operations.
+
+Warehouse, logistics and operations recruitment may be retained as supplementary research observations, but must not operate as an independent buying signal or influence current routing.

 ### Preliminary qualification outcomes
 
-| Outcome        | Interpretation                                                              |
-| -------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
-| Excellent fit  | Strong operational and commercial alignment supported by evidence.          |
-| Reasonable fit | Relevant business model with some demonstrated banking or payment needs.    |
-| Poor fit       | Limited apparent alignment with the products and services being considered. |
+| ICP outcome | Interpretation |
+|---|---|
+| `QUALIFIED` | All six mandatory ICP conditions have passed. |
+| `DISQUALIFIED` | At least one mandatory ICP condition has definitively failed. |
+| `FURTHER_RESEARCH` | No definitive failure, but one or more conditions remain unknown. |
 
 Five or more physical locations is an initial indicator of potential fit, not a mandatory threshold. High-volume single-location businesses may also qualify where their payment and collection complexity supports it.

## File Modified

- **Detected:** 2026-11-25T18:25:43+01:00
- **Path:** `architecture.md`
- **Size:** 10,422 bytes
- **SHA-256:** `d300aa1e62ce82f50c7f61468580d3b0a55e505853d127a3b1f1450481b70ccb`

### Text Change

```diff
--- previous
+++ current
@@ -137,12 +137,29 @@
 | `DISQUALIFIED` | At least one mandatory ICP condition has definitively failed. |
 | `FURTHER_RESEARCH` | No definitive failure, but one or more conditions remain unknown. |
 
-Five or more physical locations is an initial indicator of potential fit, not a mandatory threshold. High-volume single-location businesses may also qualify where their payment and collection complexity supports it.
-
-Qualified accounts are prioritised using ICP fit, signal relevance, evidence quality, recency and commercial rationale. The absence of a detected buying signal does not automatically disqualify a strong ICP fit.
-
-The detailed qualification criteria are maintained in `icp/icp.md` and `icp/signals.md`.
-
+### Fit Tier and Signal Routing
+
+Fit tier is assessed separately from mandatory ICP qualification:
+
+- `EXCELLENT_FIT`
+- `REASONABLE_FIT`
+- `UNASSESSED`
+
+Five or more physical locations is a prioritisation heuristic, not a mandatory qualification threshold. A qualifying single-location supermarket may still be included. FMCG distributors do not need retail outlets if their distribution operations are independently verified.
+
+Signal outcomes are assessed independently:
+
+- `DETECTED`
+- `NOT_DETECTED`
+- `UNVERIFIED`
+- `HISTORICAL`
+- `UNKNOWN`
+
+The two signal outcomes determine routing according to the combination matrix in `workflow.md`.
+
+The absence of a detected signal does not disqualify an otherwise qualified account. An unresolved signal search must not be interpreted as `NOT_DETECTED`.
+
+A buying signal does not establish purchase intent, dissatisfaction with an existing provider or a confirmed payment problem.
 ## 5. Data and Evidence Management
 
 The system preserves research provenance throughout account qualification.
```

@@ -85,18 +85,19 @@
 The two signal searches are independent. Both must reach terminal outcomes before final signal routing. An unresolved search must not be treated as NOT_DETECTED.
 
 ## 3. Core System Components
-| Component | Responsibility |
-|---|---|
-| Context OS | Maintains company context, ICP definitions, buying signals and operating instructions. |
-| Account discovery | Identifies businesses within the two approved target segments. |
-| Company enrichment | Establishes identity, Nigerian operations, physical operating model and other mandatory ICP evidence. |
-| Signal detection | Independently investigates finance recruitment and multi-branch expansion after mandatory ICP qualification. |
-| ICP qualification | Evaluates the six mandatory conditions using PASS, FAIL or UNKNOWN. |
-| Account prioritisation | Applies deterministic routing using both signal outcomes, evidence quality and commercial relevance. |
-| Buyer identification | Identifies relevant decision-makers and influencers after account qualification and routing. |
-| Clay | Supports account enrichment, signal research, contact preparation and workflow execution. |
-| Human review | Validates evidence, qualification, routing and contact readiness before approval. |
-| Feedback | Improves future research and qualification criteria. |
+
+| Component              | Responsibility                                                                                               |
+| ---------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
+| Context OS             | Maintains company context, ICP definitions, buying signals and operating instructions.                       |
+| Account discovery      | Identifies businesses within the two approved target segments.                                               |
+| Company enrichment     | Establishes identity, Nigerian operations, physical operating model and other mandatory ICP evidence.        |
+| Signal detection       | Independently investigates finance recruitment and multi-branch expansion after mandatory ICP qualification. |
+| ICP qualification      | Evaluates the six mandatory conditions using PASS, FAIL or UNKNOWN.                                          |
+| Account prioritisation | Applies deterministic routing using both signal outcomes, evidence quality and commercial relevance.         |
+| Buyer identification   | Identifies relevant decision-makers and influencers after account qualification and routing.                 |
+| Clay                   | Supports account enrichment, signal research, contact preparation and workflow execution.                    |
+| Human review           | Validates evidence, qualification, routing and contact readiness before approval.                            |
+| Feedback               | Improves future research and qualification criteria.                                                         |
 
 ## 4. Qualification and Prioritisation Logic
 
@@ -131,10 +132,10 @@
 
 ### Preliminary qualification outcomes
 
-| ICP outcome | Interpretation |
-|---|---|
-| `QUALIFIED` | All six mandatory ICP conditions have passed. |
-| `DISQUALIFIED` | At least one mandatory ICP condition has definitively failed. |
+| ICP outcome        | Interpretation                                                    |
+| ------------------ | ----------------------------------------------------------------- |
+| `QUALIFIED`        | All six mandatory ICP conditions have passed.                     |
+| `DISQUALIFIED`     | At least one mandatory ICP condition has definitively failed.     |
 | `FURTHER_RESEARCH` | No definitive failure, but one or more conditions remain unknown. |

+Speculative, stale or unattributable developments do not qualify as current detected signals. Its event status must remain ANNOUNCED or PLANNED, and it must not be represented as a completed or operational development. Retain relevant candidate or historical evidence with its correct status.
 
 The 180-day and 12-month windows are initial operating assumptions. Validate them during implementation.

+# ============================================================
+# 3. URL NORMALISATION AND SOURCE DEDUPLICATION
+# ============================================================
+
+def normalise_url(url):
+    """
+    Remove fragments and common tracking parameters.
+    Preserve the actual page path and meaningful query data.
+    """
+
+    if not url:
+        return None
+
+    try:
+        parts = urlsplit(url.strip())
+
+        if parts.scheme not in ("http", "https"):
+            return None
+
+        if not parts.netloc:
+            return None
+
+        tracking_parameters = {
+            "utm_source",
+            "utm_medium",
+            "utm_campaign",
+            "utm_term",
+            "utm_content",
+            "fbclid",
+            "gclid",
+        }
+
+        query_parts = []
+
+        for item in parts.query.split("&"):
+            if not item:
+                continue
+
+            key = item.split("=", 1)[0].lower()
+
+            if key not in tracking_parameters:
+                query_parts.append(item)
+
+        return urlunsplit((
+            parts.scheme.lower(),
+            parts.netloc.lower(),
+            parts.path.rstrip("/"),
+            "&".join(query_parts),
+            "",
+        ))
+
+    except Exception:
+        return None
+
+
+def make_source_id(index):
+    return f"S{index:03d}"
+
+

+

+print("MONIEPOINT MARKET RESEARCH")
+print("========================================")
+
+print(f"Research areas: {len(RESEARCH_AREAS)}")
+print(f"Maximum results per query: {MAX_RESULTS_PER_QUERY}")

 seen_urls = set()

+def normalise_url(url):
+    # remove fragments and common tracking parameters
+
+    if not url:
+        return None
+
+    try:
+        parts = urlsplit(url.strip())
+
+        if parts.scheme not in ("http", "https"):
+            return None
+
+        if not parts.netloc:
+            return None
+
+        tracking_parameters = {
+            "utm_source",
+            "utm_medium",
+            "utm_campaign",
+            "utm_term",
+            "utm_content",
+            "fbclid",
+            "gclid",
+        }
+
+        query_parts = []
+
+        for item in parts.query.split("&"):
+            if not item:
+                continue
+
+            key = item.split("=", 1)[0].lower()
+
+            if key not in tracking_parameters:
+                query_parts.append(item)
+
+        return urlunsplit((
+            parts.scheme.lower(),
+            parts.netloc.lower(),
+            parts.path.rstrip("/"),
+            "&".join(query_parts),
+            "",
+        ))
+
+    except Exception:
+        return None
+
+
+def make_source_id(index):
+    return f"S{index:03d}"
+
+

+print("========================================")
+

+print(f"Maximum results per query: {MAX_RESULTS_PER_QUERY}")

Their businesses have weathered economic downturns, currency volatility, rising inflation and shifting consumer preferences while continuing to expand into new cities and communities.\nSome pioneered organised retail long before international supermarket chains entered Nigeria, while others have disrupted the market with innovative concepts such as neighbourhood convenience stores, hard-discount grocery formats and integrated retail ecosystems that combine supermarkets, restaurants, manufacturing and shopping malls.\nThis list profiles the founders, chairmen, chief executives and managing directors behind Nigeria’s largest supermarket chains by retail stores.\nAlso Read\nSunday Egede and David Ojei – Founders Prince Ebeano\nNo of stores – 9\nSunday Egede and David Ojei have built one of Nigeria’s most recognizable retail brands through resilience, discipline and an unwavering belief in entrepreneurship.\nAlthough widely referred to as brothers, their relationship is rooted in family ties—Egede is Ojei’s maternal uncle. -    {
-        "id": "S3",
-        "title": "Nigeria Supermarket Founders Show the Scale Opportunity in Retail",
-        "url": "https://fastgist.com/2026/07/28/nigeria-supermarket-founders-retail-scale-opportunity-2026/",
-        "search_query": "Nigeria retail chains supermarket groups business expansion",
-        "evidence_type": "Full page extraction",
-        "content": "Fastgist take: Nigeria’s supermarket founders tell a bigger business story than retail shelves and store counts. Organised grocery chains sit at the point where consumer demand, supply chains, local manufacturing, property, jobs, logistics, and inflation all meet. When those chains expand, they can change how households shop and how producers reach customers.\nNairametrics profiled leaders behind some of Nigeria’s largest supermarket chains, showing how businesses that began as neighbourhood or family-led operations have become important parts of the country’s organised retail ecosystem. The report highlights names behind chains such as Prince Ebeano, SPAR Nigeria, and Justrite, and describes how the sector has grown through modern retail formats, supply-chain discipline, and customer-focused expansion.\nThe business case for organised retail in Nigeria is strong but difficult. The market is large, urbanising, and young. Consumers want convenience, predictable pricing, better product variety, and safer shopping environments. But retailers also have to manage inflation, currency volatility, rent, diesel and power costs, security, road logistics, and competition from informal markets.\nThat tension makes the supermarket sector a useful window into the Nigerian economy. When stores expand into new cities, they often create jobs directly through cashiers, floor staff, warehousing, security, procurement, and management. They also support suppliers: bakeries, farms, packaged-food makers, household-goods producers, delivery companies, and maintenance services.\nFor local manufacturers, organised retail can be valuable because it creates consistent distribution. A product that enters a growing supermarket chain can reach thousands of households without the manufacturer building its own retail network. But the relationship can be demanding. Retailers need steady supply, reliable packaging, pricing discipline, and credit terms that smaller producers may struggle to meet.\nFor shoppers, the clearest benefit is convenience. A well-run supermarket can reduce the friction of buying household essentials, comparing products, and planning weekly spending. But organised retail does not automatically mean cheaper prices. Formal stores carry costs that open markets may not. The strongest chains are those that can use scale, procurement, and logistics to protect value while still offering reliability.\nThe investment angle is also important. Retail expansion requires capital for locations, inventory, warehousing, technology, and staff. Institutional investment can help chains improve governance and digital systems, but investors will expect profitability and discipline. That can push the sector toward better standards if managed well.\nNigeria’s retail story should not be reduced to Lagos. The next stage of growth depends on whether chains can move into more cities while adapting to local incomes and shopping habits. Smaller store formats, neighbourhood convenience models, and discount-focused formats may become as important as large flagship supermarkets.\nFastgist will keep tracking this because it is finance that readers can see. Retail shows whether household demand is healthy, whether businesses can scale despite cost pressure, and whether local supply chains are becoming more formal. Behind every supermarket shelf is a wider economic network.\nSources: Nairametrics retail profile, BusinessDay Nigeria business coverage, and Reuters Africa coverage."
-    },
-    {
-        "id": "S4",
-        "title": "Meet the founders behind Nigeria’s largest supermarket chains",
-        "url": "https://bioreports.net/meet-the-founders-behind-nigerias-largest-supermarket-chains/",
-        "search_query": "Nigeria retail chains supermarket groups business expansion",
-        "evidence_type": "Full page extraction",
-        "content": "Nigeria’s supermarket industry has evolved from a handful of neighbourhood grocery stores into a multi-billion-naira retail ecosystem serving millions of consumers every day. Behind this transformation are a group of entrepreneurs and business leaders who have redefined how Nigerians shop, introducing modern retail formats, organised supply chains and customer-focused experiences in an increasingly competitive market. From family-owned businesses that began as single storefronts to retail giants operating dozens of outlets across the country, these founders and managing directors have built brands that now shape consumer spending, create thousands of jobs and support local manufacturers and farmers. Their businesses have weathered economic downturns, currency volatility, rising inflation and shifting consumer preferences while continuing to expand into new cities and communities. Some pioneered organised retail long before international supermarket chains entered Nigeria, while others have disrupted the market with innovative concepts such as neighbourhood convenience stores, hard-discount grocery formats and integrated retail ecosystems that combine supermarkets, restaurants, manufacturing and shopping malls. This list profiles the founders, chairmen, chief executives and managing directors behind Nigeria’s largest supermarket chains by retail stores. Sunday Egede and David Ojei – Founders Prince Ebeano\nNo of stores – 9 Sunday Egede and David Ojei have built one of Nigeria’s most recognizable retail brands through resilience, discipline and an unwavering belief in entrepreneurship.\nAlthough widely referred to as brothers, their relationship is rooted in family ties—Egede is Ojei’s maternal uncle. Yet, the pair have long described each other as brothers and friends, reflecting a partnership built on trust rather than titles. Egede’s entrepreneurial journey began in the early 1990s when he sold foodstuffs from a tiny room in Isolo, Lagos, using a wooden partition to separate his shop from his sleeping area. His determination laid the foundation for a thriving wholesale and retail business. Ojei followed a different path. After completing university and the National Youth Service Corps, he briefly worked in private security before leaving paid employment in 1998 to pursue business.\nDespite losing his first trading capital, he rebuilt, opened a provision store in Shomolu, and steadily expanded. In 2009, the pair formalised their partnership, creating Prince Ebeano Supermarket. Today, the business stands as one of Nigeria’s leading indigenous retail chains while providing employment opportunities for hundreds of young Nigerians.In December 2021, Prince Ebeano extended its footprint internationally by opening a store in St. Catharines, Ontario, Canada.\nSir Robert O. Anwatu –Founder/CEO Roban Stores\nNo of stores-10 Sir Robert O.\nAnwatu did not set out to build one of southeastern Nigeria’s recognised retail and hospitality businesses. Trained as an accountant, his career began in banking and public accounting before he traded balance sheets for shop shelves. After completing his National Youth Service Corps in Benin, Anwatu launched his entrepreneurial journey in 1985 with a small gift shop known as Robnat Gift. Two years later, the business was incorporated as Ultimate Gift Ltd., marking the beginning of a steady expansion. By 1989, the company had opened a second outlet in Warri before relocating operations to Enugu in 1990. The business evolved again in 1999 when Ultimate Gift was rebranded as Roban Stores.\nThat same year, Anwatu founded Roban Group, that would grow into a diversified enterprise spanning retail and hospitality through Roban Stores and Roban Hotels. Before becoming an entrepreneur, Anwatu built experience in finance, working at Union Bank then known as Barclays Bank between 1976 and 1977 before joining Charles Cole & Co., a certified public accounting firm in Little Rock, Arkansas, from 1982 to 1983. His academic background includes both bachelor’s and master’s degrees in Accountancy from the University of Arkansas. Beyond business, Anwatu has maintained an active presence in public service and civic leadership. A Knight of the Good Shepherd, he has served on Enugu State’s Local Economic Empowerment Programme (LEEMP) advisory committee, led the Enugu Chamber of Commerce as president, and also served as president of the Rotary Club of New Haven. Haresh Keswani –Founder/GMD Artee Group\nOwners of Spar Nigeria No of stores-16 Haresh Keswani is the founder and Group Managing Director of Artee Group, the diversified consumer business behind SPAR Nigeria, one of the country’s largest supermarket chains. Since establishing Artee Group in 1998 as a wholesale business, Keswani has transformed it into a major player in Nigeria’s retail, real estate, manufacturing and shopping mall sectors.\nUnder his leadership, Artee Group secured the master franchise for SPAR International, introducing the global superma"
-    },

-        "content": "FMCG® Distributions Ltd. offers a pan Nigerian logistics platform, delivering effective distribution that is trusted by market leading brand owners. If you are looking for a provider of efficient and wide- reaching distribution for your brand(s) in Nigeria, then you have come to the right place!"

-    {
-        "id": "S6",
-        "title": "Spend Management for Nigerian FMCG and Distribution Companies",
-        "url": "https://tryduplo.com/blog/spend-management-fmcg-distribution-nigeria/",
-        "search_query": "Nigeria FMCG distributors wholesale payment collection operations",
-        "evidence_type": "Full page extraction",
-        "content": "Nigeria’s FMCG sector is navigating one of its most complex operating environments in recent years. Combined profit among Nigeria’s ten leading consumer goods companies rose to N307.5 billion in Q1 2026, a 19.6% increase from the same quarter in 2025. But revenues remained largely flat, increasing only marginally from N1.76 trillion to N1.78 trillion despite widespread price increases, meaning margin improvement is coming from cost discipline rather than top-line growth.\nFMCG companies in Nigeria face a double squeeze: rising import costs driven by naira volatility and the inability to fully pass those costs to consumers whose purchasing power has weakened significantly. Several major players have responded by shifting toward local sourcing, portfolio pruning, and debt restructuring.\nFor FMCG manufacturers, distributors, and distribution companies operating in Nigeria, the businesses that protect their margins in this environment are those that manage costs with precision on every controllable dimension: supplier payments, field sales expenses, distribution fleet costs, and the FX burden on imported raw materials and inputs.\nThis guide covers the specific spend management challenges that Nigerian FMCG and distribution companies face and what a connected system changes.\nThe Spend Management Challenges Specific to Nigerian FMCG and Distribution\nHigh-volume supplier payments with tight working capital.\nFMCG distribution in Nigeria operates on thin margins and fast inventory turnover. Payment terms with principal suppliers are often short: some require payment before release of goods, others offer 7 to 30-day terms that leave little room for payment delays. Trade receivables across Nigeria’s leading FMCG companies surged to N515.3 billion in Q1 2026 from N423.4 billion in the same period of 2025, indicating that a growing portion of sales is being made on credit rather than immediate cash, putting additional pressure on the working capital position of distributors in the chain.\nFor distribution companies, the working capital pressure translates directly into spend management pressure: supplier payments must be executed accurately and on time to maintain supply allocation, while incoming payments from retailers are increasingly delayed by credit terms.\nFX costs on imported raw materials and inputs.\nNigeria imports over USD 10 billion worth of food annually, and FMCG companies face elevated costs on imported inputs as the naira exchange rate stabilizes but FX shortages persist. In 2025, Nestlé Nigeria prioritized paying off USD 20 million of inter-company foreign debt early to limit risk from further currency depreciation, while Cadbury Nigeria swapped USD 7.7 million of debt with parent company Mondelez in return for equities\nFor FMCG manufacturers sourcing imported ingredients, packaging, or equipment, the FX cost on every purchase is a directly controllable expense that most businesses have not actively optimized. Banks apply FX margins above the interbank rate without disclosure. A manufacturer making ten USD-denominated supplier payments per month is absorbing that margin ten times, with no systematic way to reduce it unless they actively manage their FX strategy.\nField sales expense management across a distributed workforce.\nNigerian FMCG distribution relies heavily on field sales representatives, van sales operators, and trade marketing teams operating across multiple states simultaneously. These teams incur daily expenses: fuel, accommodation for out-of-Lagos routes, market activation costs, and trade promotions funded at the point of sale.\nWithout structured mobile expense capture and approval, field sales expenses are managed through cash advances that are reconciled weeks later, often with missing receipts, inconsistent categorization, and limited ability to track costs against specific routes, territories, or campaigns.\nMulti-depot spend visibility without centralized control.\nA distribution company with depots in Lagos, Ibadan, Kano, and Port Harcourt is managing spending across four locations simultaneously. Each depot incurs operational expenses, fleet maintenance costs, and local vendor payments. Without a connected spend management system, the consolidated cost picture is only visible at month-end when data from all depots has been manually aggregated, by which time it is too late to intervene on overruns that have already occurred.\nHow Spend Management Addresses Nigerian FMCG and Distribution Cost Challenges\nFive Spend Management Priorities for Nigerian FMCG and Distribution Companies\nPriority 1: Automate high-volume supplier payment cycles.\nFMCG distributors make payments to dozens of suppliers on recurring cycles. Processing these individually, each with manual authorization and bank transfer initiation, creates processing overhead that grows with the business and introduces delays that damage supplier standing. Bulk payment capability, paying up to 500 suppliers in a single run with automated"
-    },
-    {
-        "id": "S7",
-        "title": "Top 20 FMCG Companies in Nigeria in 2026 - Duplo",
-        "url": "https://tryduplo.com/blog/top-20-fmcg-companies-in-nigeria/",
-        "search_query": "Nigeria FMCG distributors wholesale payment collection operations",
-        "evidence_type": "Full page extraction",
-        "content": "The FMCG sector is a vital contributor to Nigeria’s GDP, being the fourth-largest sector in the economy. It creates employment for more than three million people.\nNigeria’s consumer goods industry has continued to demonstrate resilience in 2026. The listed consumer goods sector crossed NGN 3.5 trillion in combined revenue in the first half of 2026, with the top 10 companies generating combined profits of NGN 601.74 billion during the period. BUA Foods alone accounted for 49.47% of that combined profit, recording NGN 292.26 billion in profit after tax and a net profit margin of 38.20%, the highest in the sector.\nThe results reflect a sector that is learning to do more with less: reformulating products to incorporate local inputs, investing in operational efficiency, and building financial discipline into the distribution chain. For distributors working with these companies, the pressure on margins makes payment and cash flow management more consequential than ever.\nThe various consumer goods companies in Nigeria have been broadly grouped into three categories:\n- Household care\n- Personal care\n- Food and beverages\nThe Current State of Nigeria’s FMCG Market\nStrong Profits Despite Flat Revenue Growth\nNigeria’s top 10 listed consumer goods companies generated more than NGN 3.5 trillion in combined revenue in H1 2026 and NGN 601.74 billion in combined profit after tax. The growth is coming from cost controls, price increases, and operational efficiency rather than from increased consumer spending volumes. Revenue growth remained largely flat across many companies despite widespread price increases, indicating that volume pressure from weakening purchasing power is offsetting the benefit of higher prices per unit.\nRising Credit Sales Exposure\nOne of the most significant trends in Nigeria’s FMCG sector in 2026 is the surge in trade receivables. At Nestlé Nigeria, trade receivables surged almost fivefold to NGN 26.9 billion in Q1 2026 from NGN 5.4 billion in the same period of 2025. At NASCON Allied Industries, receivables represented more than 150% of quarterly revenue. Weakening consumer spending power is forcing FMCG companies to extend more credit to their distribution networks, which increases the pressure on distributors to manage collections and cash flow tightly.\nLocal Sourcing and Cost Adaptation\nTo cut the rising costs of imports, FMCG companies are reformulating products and shifting toward more local inputs. Unilever has pivoted to brands that incorporate more local sourcing. This trend is reducing forex dependency but requires distributors to stay close to product changes and adapt their sales positioning accordingly. Our FMCG Payments Playbook covers this extensively.\nTop 20 FMCG Companies in Nigeria\nAs Africa’s largest consumer-goods producer, Nigeria has more than 100 listed FMCG companies. Selecting the top names from this list wasn’t easy. Using the criteria below, we narrowed it down to the final twenty.\n- Revenue\n- Product Range\n- Cross-country Penetration\n- The popularity of product range\nIf you are looking for some of the best in this industry, these are the top 20 FMCG companies in Nigeria:\n- Dangote Group\n- Nestlé Nigeria Plc\n- BUA Foods Plc\n- Nigerian Breweries Plc\n- Unilever Nigeria Plc\n- Guinness Nigeria Plc\n- Cadbury Nigeria Plc\n- Flour Mills of Nigeria (FMN)\n- Honeywell Flour Mills Plc\n- PZ Cussons Nigeria Plc\n- NASCON Allied Industries Plc\n- FrieslandCampina Wamco Nigeria Plc\n- Dufil Prima Foods (Indomie)\n- Promasidor Nigeria Limited\n- Chi Limited\n- Coca-Cola HBC Nigeria Limited\n- Seven-Up Bottling Company\n- International Breweries Plc\n- Reckitt Benckiser Nigeria\n- UAC of Nigeria Plc\nEvery month, new companies spring up, and existing ones release new products. Each has its own list of products, but how do you know which ones are the best for your distribution business? What stands them out among consumers?\nOne word – Research! Take time to research each company thoroughly and understand how their products perform in your area.\n👉 Read more: How to Start a Successful FMCG Distribution Business in Nigeria, Cost Control and Efficiency in FMCG Operations: 4 Strategies to Consider\nThe Payment Challenge Every FMCG Distributor Knows\nDistributing for any of the companies above means collecting from dozens of retailers, paying principal suppliers on tight terms, and reconciling transactions that arrive without reference numbers throughout every working day. With trade receivables rising sharply across the sector, the pressure on distributors to collect efficiently and pay reliably has never been higher.\nMost Nigerian FMCG distributors are managing this across spreadsheets, multiple bank portals, and WhatsApp approval chains. A distributor collecting from 200 active retailers making two to three payments per month is managing 400 to 600 individual payment matching exercises every month. At 15 minutes per unmatched transfer, that is up to 150 hours of finance team time every month on a process that"
-    },

-        "title": "About us – FMCG Distributions Ltd",
-        "url": "https://www.fmcgdistribution.org/about-us/",

-        "evidence_type": "Full page extraction",
-        "content": "Founded in 2011, FMCG Distributions Ltd. is a results driven Distribution, Sales and Marketing company operating throughout Nigeria. We service multiple sales channels including Wholesale, Traditional Retail, Modern Trade and HORECA.\nWe employ in excess of 400 people in frontline Distribution and Sales roles ably supported by the traditional Back Office roles.\nCurrently present in 30 of the 36 states, with plans to achieve total coverage by year 2026."

-    {
-        "id": "S9",
-        "title": "TOP 10 B2B FINTECH BUSINESSES IN NIGERIA - TechCabal",
-        "url": "https://techcabal.com/2026/06/16/fintech-businesses-in-nigeria/",
-        "search_query": "Nigeria business banking POS settlement competitors fintech",
-        "evidence_type": "Full page extraction",
-        "content": "The landscape of Nigeria’s business fintech space is highly competitive, with millions of business owners and high-scaling teams constantly looking for platforms to optimise their daily operations.\nFintech has developed over time from basic money transfers to a whole financial infrastructure, assisting businesses with payment support, operational management, and the removal of payment bottlenecks.\nSome of the most cutting-edge systems created especially to maintain the efficient operation of corporate budgets are being introduced in 2026. The top ten business fintech platforms in Nigeria at the moment are as follows:\n1. Platnova Business\nPlatnova Business addresses the one problem most Nigerian companies don’t talk about enough: the daily friction of managing multi-currency cash flow through legacy banking infrastructure never built for cross-border operations.\nBusinesses are provided with dedicated NGN, USD, GBP, and EUR wallets at the click of a button across departments with Platnova Business, without the hassle of multiple correspondent banking relationships or the need to reconcile FX conversions manually. Their global card infrastructure offers configurable spend limits, controlled merchant acceptance, and clean developer API documentation for businesses looking to grow and scale. The outcome is a fully automated spend workflow that, for any scaling business in 2026, is less of a nice-to-have and more of a competitive requirement.\n2. Moniepoint Business\nMoniepoint is the backbone of physical commerce in Nigeria. Their POS terminals dominate storefronts from Lagos Island to Kano, backed by high uptime, instant settlements, and built-in payroll and inventory tools. For SMEs processing high-volume in-person transactions, Moniepoint remains the most battle-tested option on the market.\n3. Paystack (by Stripe)\nPaystack operates primarily as an online payment gateway for websites and mobile applications. Their service relies on providing standard developer APIs and documentation for businesses needing card, transfer, or mobile money checkout setups. Recently, the company expanded its scope beyond digital processing by utilizing a microfinance banking license to offer business accounts, structured deposits, and compliance tracking directly through its dashboard\n4. OPay Business\nOPay’s consumer network feeds directly into its merchant ecosystem, and that’s a more significant advantage than it sounds. A sprawling agent banking network, 99.9% transaction uptime, and zero-fee intra-wallet transfers make OPay Business a serious option for high-volume retail merchants where processing speed and low transaction overhead aren’t negotiable.\n5. Flutterwave\nFlutterwave operates at an enterprise scale and also functions primarily as an international payment gateway connecting African merchants to global networks. Their platform provides processing across multiple currencies and countries simultaneously, serving businesses that handle cross-border customer collections. Earlier this year, the company secured a local microfinance banking license in Nigeria to internalize elements of its payout chain and speed up merchant settlements.\n6. Kuda Business\nKuda has evolved from a consumer neobank into a functional workspace tool for digital-first teams and freelancers. Zero-maintenance business accounts, granular spending analytics, and internal invoicing tools give smaller teams clear cash flow visibility without the operational overhead of traditional corporate banking.\n7. Remita\nRemita operates as a major transaction processor managing backend payment flows for large-scale operations. Over 150,000 businesses and hundreds of financial institutions use its setup to process corporate payroll, taxes, pensions, and automated collections. Holding licenses from the CBN for payment switching and super-agent services, the platform handles significant volume by bridging public and private sector payment networks\n8. Grey Business\nGrey built its reputation solving the foreign account problem for Nigerian professionals, and that same infrastructure powers their business product. For remote-first agencies, tech startups, and creator-led businesses billing international clients regularly, Grey’s dedicated foreign virtual accounts with competitive FX conversion rates offer a cleaner alternative to routing payments through traditional correspondent banking.\n9. BudPay\nBudPay provides standard online payment gateways and payout APIs for digital setups and early-stage companies. Their service focuses on handling local card processing and cross-border transfers without heavy setup requirements\n10. PalmPay Business\nPalmPay’s real asset is its user base of tens of millions of active wallet holders representing real, accessible purchasing power for consumer-facing merchants. Combined with reliable POS settlement and instant transaction processing, PalmPay is a smart acquisition channel for retail businesses trying to capture Nigeria’s mobile-first co"
-    },
-    {
-        "id": "S10",
-        "title": "The 2026 Fintech Power List: Top 10 Payment Companies in Nigeria.",
-        "url": "https://budpay.com/ng/blog/the-2026-fintech-power-list-top-10-payment-companies-in-nigeria-563",
-        "search_query": "Nigeria business banking POS settlement competitors fintech",
-        "evidence_type": "Full page extraction",
-        "content": "The Nigerian fintech landscape has undergone a seismic shift. If 2022 was the year of the “Unicorn,” then 2026 is the year of the “Utility.” No longer content with just burning VC cash, the top payment companies in Nigeria have matured into profitable, infrastructure-heavy institutions that rival traditional Tier-1 banks.\nAccording to the latest CBN National Financial Inclusion Strategy (NFIS) Report for 2026, digital payment penetration has hit an all-time high of 82% among the adult population. From the bustling markets of Kano to the tech hubs of Yaba, these ten companies are the engines driving Africa’s largest economy.\nFlutterwave remains the undisputed heavyweight champion. By 2026, the company will have successfully moved past its previous regulatory hurdles to become a model of African corporate governance. Their “Barter” evolution into a full-scale commerce engine allows Nigerian merchants to sync their inventory directly with global marketplaces like Amazon and Alibaba.\nOPay’s green-and-white branding is more ubiquitous in 2026 than most bank branches. By leveraging a network of over 1.2 million agents, OPay has solved the “last mile” problem of financial inclusion. Their super-app now includes AI-driven micro-lending, which analyzes transaction history to provide instant credit to petty traders.\nMoniepoint is the silent giant of the Nigerian economy. In 2026, it is rare to find a physical store in Lagos or Abuja without a Moniepoint POS terminal. Their pivot from a pure agency banking model to a full-service business bank has allowed them to capture the “middle market” that traditional banks often ignore.\nThe “Grandfather of Fintech” is still very much in the game. In 2026, Interswitch has focused on the “rails.” They provide the backend switching technology for almost all Nigerian banks. Their Verve card has officially overtaken international competitors in local transaction volume due to lower scheme fees and better local support.\nPalmPay has mastered the “gamification” of finance. In 2026, users don’t just pay bills; they earn “PalmPoints” that can be used for data, electricity, and even transport. Their aggressive marketing and high-yield savings products (PalmForce) have made them the preferred choice for Gen Z and Millennials.\nPaystack continues to provide the cleanest API documentation in the market. In 2026, they have expanded deeply into “Paystack Commerce,” a Shopify-like experience for Nigerians. Their integration with Stripe’s global network means a Nigerian developer can start a business and accept payments from a customer in Tokyo as easily as one in Port Harcourt.\nKuda has transitioned from the “Bank of the Free” to the “Bank of the Bold.” In 2026, their focus is on Kuda Business, offering automated tax filing for freelancers and high-interest corporate accounts. Their physical “Experience Centres” have replaced traditional bank branches, offering tech support and lounge access to premium members.\nPaga remains the most resilient fintech in the North and rural South. Their 2026 strategy involves “offline payments” via USSD and sound-wave technology, allowing users to pay even without an internet connection. They remain the primary partner for the Nigerian government’s social investment programs.\nIf you are paying the Federal Government of Nigeria in 2026, you are using Remita. Owned by SystemSpecs, this platform has saved the government trillions in leaked revenue. Their new Remita HR module has become the standard for public and private sector salary disbursements.\nThe newest entrant on the list, BudPay, has skyrocketed to a $400M valuation in just 18 months. The secret? Fast Settlements. They allow Nigerian businesses to pay international suppliers using Naira, which is instantly converted to USDC on the backend, bypassing the traditional 3-day wait time of legacy banks."
