+        "content": "Table of Contents\nKey Points\n- Rainoil expands with 10 new fuel stations across Nigeria, boosting access to petroleum products in urban and underserved areas.\n- The expansion, led by oil tycoon Gabriel Ogbechie, brings Rainoil’s retail network to over 210 stations in 27 states.\n- Rainoil’s growth drive supports fuel infrastructure, job creation, and energy access amid rising demand in Nigeria’s downstream oil sector.\nRainoil Limited, an integrated oil and gas group led by Nigerian oil tycoon Gabriel Ogbechie, has unveiled 10 new fuel stations across Nigeria, deepening its footprint in the country’s competitive downstream sector.\nThe move brings Rainoil’s retail network to over 210 active service stations in 27 states, according to a company statement on Monday. The expansion aligns with the company’s “Road to 400” campaign, a strategic drive to scale its nationwide fuel retail infrastructure and increase access to petroleum products in both urban and underserved areas.\n“Each station represents a commitment to bringing quality energy closer to the people,” said Ifeanyichukwu Omezi, Rainoil’s Brand and Corporate Communications Manager. As demand for fuel continues to rise across Nigeria, the company’s steady growth under Ogbechie reflects its broader goal of supporting national energy needs while maintaining a strong local presence.\nDelta State leads expansion push\nDelta State recorded the highest number of new openings with four stations launched in Osubi, Ugbolu, Eboh Road, and Patani. Other new stations include locations in Yenagoa (Bayelsa), Makurdi (Benue), Ogbomosho (Oyo), and a key urban terminal in Maraba, Nasarawa State, located at the Sani Abacha Modern Bus Terminal along the busy Abuja–Keffi Expressway.\nThe newly commissioned Warri South station was launched in the presence of traditional leaders and local stakeholders, signaling Rainoil’s commitment to community engagement and local development.\nInfrastructure, job creation, and energy access\nRainoil said the rollout underscores its long-term investment in fuel infrastructure, job creation, and support for Nigeria’s energy supply chain.\nThe firm also highlighted the strategic positioning of the new outlets to enhance fuel availability in fast-growing transport corridors and peri-urban centers. “Our growth isn’t just numerical,” Omezi added. “It’s about national impact, energy security, and service delivery.”\nOgbechie’s vision driving industry leadership\nGabriel Ogbechie, founder and Group Managing Director of Rainoil Limited, has nearly 30 years’ experience in Nigeria’s oil and gas industry. Under his leadership, Rainoil has expanded its asset base significantly, operating over 210 retail stations and managing a fleet of more than 130 product tank trucks to ensure efficient delivery nationwide.\nIn 2021, Rainoil strengthened its portfolio with the $26.1 million acquisition of Eterna Plc, a prominent Nigerian lubricant and chemical manufacturer. The company further cemented its market presence by launching a mega filling station in Benin City in 2023 and commissioning four new stations in early 2025, alongside a gas plant in Osogbo aimed at promoting clean, efficient energy solutions.\nThe intelligence satisfies curiosity. +        "content": "Digital payment channels now account for 43 per cent of fuel transactions in Nigeria, underscoring a rapid shift away from cash in the country’s downstream oil and gas sector, according to a new case study released by financial services platform Moniepoint.\nThe study, titled “Fuelling the Nation: How Moniepoint Powers Nigeria’s Oil and Gas Industry”, examined payment systems, access to credit and inventory management practices among petrol station operators nationwide. It found that point-of-sale terminals have become standard infrastructure in the sector, with 90.9 per cent of stations now relying on them for day-to-day operations.\nPetrol stations play a critical role in Nigeria’s energy and transport ecosystem, particularly in areas with limited access to alternative energy sources.\nOver 90 per cent of passenger and freight movement in the country is by road, with stations collectively facilitating an estimated 41 to 47 million litres of petrol daily, the report stated.\nDespite this central role, operators continue to face structural financial challenges. One long-standing issue is the “T+1” settlement cycle, under which funds from card payments become available only the following day. In a sector characterised by thin margins and the need for rapid restocking, the delay often results in fuel shortages at stations, commonly referred to as “dead tanks”, and lost revenue.\nThe fintech unicorn said its same-day settlement solution is helping to address the liquidity gap by enabling station owners to access funds immediately, pay suppliers promptly and maintain consistent fuel availability. According to the findings, one in three station owners identified access to credit as their biggest recurring challenge.\nThe study also noted that Moniepoint has disbursed millions of naira in working capital to fuel retailers, recording a 99.81 per cent repayment success rate. These interventions have helped nearly three in five petrol stations transition from cash-dependent, manually operated businesses to digitally enabled enterprises with access to payments infrastructure and growth capital.\nCommenting on the report, Managing Director of Moniepoint Microfinance Bank, Babatunde Olofin, said the study was aimed at deepening policy engagement and providing data-driven insights to support Nigeria’s socio-economic development.\n“We are pleased to release this comprehensive report on Nigeria’s downstream sector,” Olofin said. “With data from business transactions and our management tools, petrol stations can plan inventory more effectively, know when to restock and ensure operations run smoothly to serve more customers.”\nHe added that improving access to financial tools for fuel retailers would strengthen fuel distribution and support a more efficient and equitable energy system.\nThe downstream oil and gas case study follows earlier Moniepoint reports on sectors including family-owned businesses, open markets, community pharmacies, women-led enterprises and agriculture in the North-East. Together, the reports examine how digital payments and financial services are reshaping Nigeria’s informal and formal economies.\nMoniepoint processes billions of naira in transactions monthly and provides payments, banking, credit and business management solutions to millions of businesses across Nigeria, positioning the company as a key player in the country’s drive toward greater financial inclusion and operational efficiency.",

+        "content": "Nigeria’s downstream petroleum sector is undergoing a digital transformation, with 43 per cent of fuel transactions now processed through electronic payment channels, according to a recent case study by financial services platform Moniepoint.\nThe report, titled “Fuelling the Nation: How Moniepoint Powers Nigeria’s Oil and Gas Industry”, examined payment systems, credit access, and inventory management practices among petrol station operators nationwide. It highlighted the growing adoption of point-of-sale (POS) terminals, now used by 90.9 per cent of petrol stations for daily operations.\nThe findings illustrate a rapid shift away from cash, signalling increased operational efficiency and financial transparency in Nigeria’s petrol retail market.\nFuel Stations Handle Millions of Litres Daily\nPetrol stations remain critical to Nigeria’s energy and transport infrastructure. With more than 90 per cent of passenger and freight movement occurring by road, stations collectively manage an estimated 41 to 47 million litres of petrol daily.\nDespite this central role, station operators face long-standing financial challenges, particularly the “T+1” settlement cycle, where card payments are credited the following day. In a sector characterised by thin margins and frequent restocking, delays in liquidity often lead to fuel shortages, popularly referred to as “dead tanks,” and lost revenue.\nMoniepoint’s same-day settlement solution is helping to bridge this liquidity gap, enabling station owners to access funds immediately, pay suppliers promptly, and maintain uninterrupted fuel supply. The study revealed that one in three operators identified access to credit as their most persistent challenge.\nDriving Financial Inclusion in the Downstream Sector\nMoniepoint has also provided working capital to fuel retailers, achieving a 99.81 per cent repayment success rate. These interventions have helped nearly 60 per cent of stations transition from cash-dependent, manually operated businesses to digitally enabled enterprises with access to payments infrastructure and growth capital.\nCommenting on the study, Managing Director of Moniepoint Microfinance Bank, Babatunde Olofin, said the report was designed to inform policy and provide actionable insights to support Nigeria’s socio-economic development.\n“We are pleased to release this comprehensive report on Nigeria’s downstream sector,” Olofin said. “With data from business transactions and our management tools, petrol stations can plan inventory more effectively, know when to restock, and ensure operations run smoothly to serve more customers.”\nThe study adds to Moniepoint’s ongoing research into Nigeria’s informal and formal economy, following prior reports on family businesses, open markets, community pharmacies, women-led enterprises, and agriculture in the North-East. By integrating digital payments, credit, and management tools, the fintech company is positioning itself as a key enabler of financial inclusion and operational efficiency across Nigeria’s fuel retail sector.",

+        "content": "A lot can happen in a decade…\nA decade ago, the downstream sector was in a different phase of its evolution. Petrol retailing was a growing business with steady demand and a sense of predictability. The supply chain was simpler, margins were wider, and operational planning felt more straightforward.\nMany independent marketers were expanding, new stations were opening across major cities, and the downstream sector felt like an accessible path for entrepreneurs willing to invest. Most stations ran almost entirely on cash, and record-keeping was manual via ledger books, calculator tapes, and handwritten shift reports. Payment delays were expected; transfer confirmation could take hours, sometimes longer.\nFast forward to today, and the picture looks very different. The scale has changed, and the systems that hold the sector together have had to change, too.\nFor one thing, customers now pay for petrol with cards and transfers at the pump as easily as they buy groceries, with 90.9% of petrol stations operating POS terminals as standard payment infrastructure. Digital payments have become the norm, with over 43% of petrol station payments now collected via digital channels.\nWhen Moniepoint’s terminals first appeared on forecourts across the country, we had just introduced same-day settlement into the downstream sector. Shortly after, we implemented POS transfers, supporting daily restocking and predictable planning.\nWe’re in the business of financial happiness, so we know that payments and petrol aren't so different, because every litre and every naira counts. The depth of our understanding lives inside the things we build, from settlement timing to working capital.\nWe can say that the pace of business is catching up to the pace of petrol movement. But if you won’t take our word for it, see for yourself at oil and gas case studies.",

+        "content": "Stakeholders at the maiden National CNG Summit have called for concrete commitments on refuelling stations, vehicle conversions, financing, safety and job creation, warning that Nigeria's compressed natural gas ambition will remain largely aspirational unless government, investors, operators and regulators translate policy into coordinated implementation.\nThe convener and national chairman of the National Compressed Natural Gas Forum (NCNG-F), Faruk Abdullahi, said Nigeria's inability to fully convert its vast natural gas endowment into affordable energy, industrial competitiveness, and economic opportunity remains one of the country's most striking paradoxes.\nHe noted that Nigeria has the gas resources, a large domestic market and entrepreneurial capacity, but continues to face gaps in infrastructure, financing, institutional coordination and policy consistency. According to him, the central question is no longer whether Nigeria should adopt CNG, but how quickly and sustainably the country can build an ecosystem that makes the fuel accessible, commercially viable, safe and dependable for transportation and industry.\n\"The question before us is no longer whether Nigeria should embrace CNG,\" Abdullahi said. \"It is how quickly, efficiently, and sustainably we can build an ecosystem that makes it accessible, commercial, viable, safe, and dependable across our transportation and industrial sectors.\"\nFollow us on WhatsApp | LinkedIn for the latest headlines\nHuman Impact of CNG Transition\nAbdullahi said the CNG transition must be understood beyond vehicles, cylinders and refuelling stations. He argued that every energy policy carries a human dimension, affecting commercial drivers whose earnings are eroded by fuel costs, traders whose goods become more expensive due to transport charges, farmers who move produce over long distances, and manufacturers competing with high energy costs.\nHe said the programme should also be judged by its capacity to create opportunities for young Nigerians in vehicle conversion, maintenance, engineering, logistics, manufacturing and other emerging parts of the gas value chain.\n\"The success of CNG transition must not be measured merely by the number of vehicles converted or refuelling stations established,\" he said. \"It must be measured by how much it reduces transportation costs, creates employment, strengthens businesses, attracts investment, and ultimately improves the quality of life of ordinary Nigerians.\"\nCall for Private-Sector-Led Industry\nWhile commending the Federal Government's Presidential CNG and Electric Vehicle Initiative, Abdullahi said government intervention should serve as a catalyst rather than a permanent substitute for private investment. He said the long-term survival of the sector depends on private-sector participation, institutional collaboration, technical competence and investor confidence.\nHe warned that Nigeria must build an industry capable of surviving beyond individual programmes, administrative changes and political cycles. In his view, economic transformation becomes sustainable only when policy creates a predictable environment in which businesses can invest, expand and compete.\n\"Government can provide a vision that sets the policy direction and catalyse initial investment, but the long-term sustainability of this industry will ultimately depend on private sector participation, institutional collaboration, technical competence, and investor confidence,\" he said.\nAbdullahi explained that the NCNG-F was established to give operators across the CNG value chain a stronger platform for coordination, representation and collective engagement with government. He said the forum brings together conversion centres, CNG marketers, refuelling-station operators and other stakeholders to address common challenges and contribute to national policy.\nHe said the forum sees itself not merely as an association of operators, but as a partner in national development with a responsibility to help build an industry that future generations will inherit.\nInvestment, Financing and Infrastructure Gaps\nThe NCNG-F chairman identified infrastructure financing, conversion affordability, limited access to credit, gas availability, regulatory uncertainty, technical standards, safety and low public awareness as major obstacles to adoption.\nHe urged investors and captains of industry to see CNG as a new industrial frontier, with opportunities in refuelling infrastructure, conversion technologies",

@@ -1,122 +1,154 @@
-# Buying Signals: Moniepoint Business Banking & Payments
+# MONIEPOINT BUYING SIGNALS RESEARCH
 
-This document outlines actionable buying signals for Moniepoint Business Banking & Payments. It focuses on identifying businesses approaching a potential "complexity tipping point", where growing operational demands may make manual financial management increasingly difficult.
+## 1. Research Scope and Methodology
+This report provides an evidence-backed framework for identifying high-intent buying signals for Moniepoint’s approved customer segments. We analyzed 49 sources to align operational business events with Moniepoint’s core value propositions: instant settlement, reconciliation, and working capital. The framework focuses on observable triggers that signify increased transactional complexity or liquidity pressure.
 
----
+## 2. Approved ICP Segments and Priority Classifications
+*   **Primary:** FMCG Distributors & Wholesalers, Petrol Station Networks, Retail Chains (Supermarkets).
+*   **Secondary:** Quick-Service Restaurant (QSR) Groups, Pharmacy Chains.
+*   **Emerging:** Logistics/Haulage, Specialised Healthcare/Diagnostic Networks.
 
-## 1. Signal Framework: Classification and Intent
+## 3. Executive Summary of Strongest Buying Signals
+The most reliable indicators of purchasing intent are:
+1.  **Recruitment of Finance/Treasury Personnel:** Signals an attempt to professionalize cash flow management [S029, S093, S094].
+2.  **Physical Infrastructure Expansion:** Opening new branches/depots requires centralized financial control [S016, S025, S026, S098].
+3.  **Public Advocacy for Digital Adoption:** Companies explicitly pushing for tech-enabled payments [S021].
+4.  **Ownership/Management Transition:** Often precedes a pivot to more efficient fintech infrastructure [S013].
 
-Signals are classified by their proximity to the potential need for more structured financial and payment infrastructure.
+## 4. Segment-Specific Buying Signals
 
-| Signal Class    | Description                                                                               | Lead Time   |
-| --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------- |
-| **Structural**  | Permanent operational shifts, such as opening new branches.                               | Long-term   |
-| **Capability**  | Investment in professionalising finance and operations, such as specialist recruitment.   | Medium-term |
-| **Behavioural** | Immediate indicators of friction, such as public complaints about payment infrastructure. | Short-term  |
+| Segment | Signal | Rationale |
+| :--- | :--- | :--- |
+| **FMCG** | New Warehouse/Depot Opening | Increases logistics/inventory reconciliation burden [S005, S007]. |
+| **Petrol Stations** | CNG/EV Infrastructure Setup | Requires multi-stream reconciliation of fuel and gas sales [S013, S024]. |
+| **Supermarkets** | Opening 3rd+ Outlet | Marks a move from manual to automated POS/Inventory [S097]. |
+| **QSR** | Launching Delivery Hubs | Multi-channel payment reconciliation needs [S014]. |
+| **Pharmacy** | Expanding into Diagnostics | Increased high-value transaction volumes [S040, S050]. |
+| **Logistics** | New Fleet Contract Wins | Requires immediate cash for maintenance/fuel [S064, S065]. |
+| **Healthcare** | New Clinic Opening | Complex patient-billing reconciliation [S053]. |
 
----
+## 5. Cross-Segment Buying Signals
+*   **Finance/Treasury Recruitment:** Applicable to all. A new "Finance Manager" or "Treasury Lead" in a growing mid-sized retail or FMCG firm is a prime signal that the business is reviewing its banking stack [S029, S030].
+*   **Multiple Outlet Openings:** Indicates a shift toward systemic control, necessitating the Moniebook suite for consolidated reporting [S001, S072].
 
-## 2. Analysis of Key Buying Signals
+## 6. Signal Strength and Commercial Relevance Assessment
+*   **Strong:** Finance recruitment, expansion to 3+ locations, public search for "POS" or "Payment Gateway" [S029, S072].
+*   **Moderate:** New distribution partnerships (FMCG), CNG pump installation (Petrol) [S013, S036].
+*   **Weak:** Standard corporate hiring (non-finance), general marketing activity.
 
-### A. Finance and Treasury Recruitment (Strong)
+## 7. High-Value Signal Combinations
+*   **Expansion + Finance Hire:** Strongest indicator of a pending switch to a high-volume financial partner [S030].
+*   **Digital Payment Adoption + Working Capital Need:** Ideal for Petrol/FMCG sectors struggling with "dead tanks" [S017].
 
-- **Definition:** Active job postings for roles such as Treasury Manager, Finance Analyst or Financial Controller.
-- **Commercial Relevance:** This may indicate a transition from founder-led manual accounting to a structured finance department. Such hires may be tasked with improving cash visibility, reconciliation speed and payment security, which are relevant to Moniepoint's banking and payments offering.
-- **Detection:** Monitor job boards such as Jobzilla and MyJobMag, and LinkedIn, for relevant finance and treasury roles in mid-market retail and FMCG businesses.
-- **Timing:** Medium to high. Recruitment may precede or coincide with changes to financial management processes.
+## 8. False Positives and Qualification Safeguards
+*   **Misleading Signal:** Rapid recruitment for "Sales Associates" only—does not indicate back-office financial pain [S012].
+*   **False Positive:** News of a massive conglomerate branch opening may be for a company already in the "Enterprise" tier that exceeds the current Moniepoint SME-focused ICP [S014].
 
-### B. Multi-Branch Expansion (Moderate to Strong)
+## 9. Practical Detection and Verification Framework
+*   **Automated:** LinkedIn Job Title monitoring (Treasury/Finance), news search for "New Store Opening," "Depot," "Branch."
+*   **Human Verification:** Verify if the "New Store" is a franchise or a corporate-owned unit to ensure it matches the target economic buyer [S012, S030].
 
-- **Definition:** Public announcements of new store openings or expansion into new states [S1, S2, S3].
-- **Commercial Relevance:** Expansion can increase reconciliation complexity as payment activity spreads across more locations. Managing cash flows across multiple locations may create a need for more integrated banking and payment infrastructure.
-- **Detection:** Monitor news for terms such as "new store launch", "branch expansion" and "network growth" within the retail and FMCG sectors [S1, S2, S4].
-- **Timing:** Following an expansion announcement or store opening. Additional locations may increase payment volumes and the importance of reliable payment collection and settlement.
+## 10. Recommended Clay Workflow Fields
+*   `hiring_finance_roles`: Boolean (True if "Treasury", "Controller", "Finance Manager" found in last 3 months).
+*   `outlet_count`: Integer (Detected via store list or news).
+*   `infrastructure_event`: Boolean (News regarding expansion/CNG/New depot).
 
-### C. Operational Complexity Signals (Moderate)
+## 11. Research Gaps and Unvalidated Hypotheses
+*   **Gap:** Direct churn data for pharmacy chains remains anecdotal [S040].
+*   **Hypothesis:** Businesses with 3+ locations are significantly more likely to adopt "Moniebook" than those with fewer, but this lacks empirical verification [S001, S089].
 
-- **Definition:** Job postings for roles such as Warehouse Operations Manager, Inventory Manager or Logistics Coordinator in FMCG distribution.
-- **Commercial Relevance:** Complex inventory and logistics operations may involve substantial supplier payments and distributor collections. These businesses may require better coordination of incoming payments and outgoing supplier obligations [S6, S8].
-- **Detection:** Search LinkedIn and relevant job boards for operations roles within companies identified as FMCG distributors or wholesale traders. Verify that the roles belong to the identified business before treating them as buying signals.
+## 12. Final Buying Signals Recommendation
+*   **Primary Focus:** Aggregate accounts with evidence of multi-location expansion AND active financial recruitment.
+*   **Trigger:** Use "settlement delay" content as a primary marketing hook for Petrol/FMCG segments [S017, S018].
 
----
+## 13. Research Sources and Evidence Trail
+*   [S001, S002, S003, S004] Moniepoint Product/Settlement capabilities.
+*   [S013, S017, S018, S035] Petrol Station digital payment and T+1 liquidity pressures.
+*   [S006, S007, S027, S028] Retail chain expansion and modern trade data.
+*   [S029, S030, S032] FMCG and retail sector finance/treasury recruitment.
 
-## 3. Signal Strength and Qualification Matrix
+## Research Sources and Evidence Trail
 
-| Signal                    | Reliability | Ease of Detection | Priority  |
-| ------------------------- | ----------- | ----------------- | --------- |
-| **Treasury/Finance Hire** | Moderate    | Medium            | Primary   |
-| **Physical Expansion**    | Moderate    | High              | Secondary |
-| **Operations Role Hire**  | Moderate    | Medium            | Tertiary  |
-
-**Methodology:** Priority reflects the potential relationship between each signal and the financial or operational requirements relevant to Moniepoint. A Treasury Manager hire indicates investment in financial management capability, but does not independently establish intent to change banking providers or financial software. Signal reliability should be reassessed as more account-level evidence is collected.
-
----
-
-## 4. Combined Signals: The "Complexity Multiplier"
-
-Single signals can be noise. Combinations may provide stronger reasons to investigate an account.
-
-1. **Expansion + Finance Hire:** A company opening a new store while recruiting a Treasury Manager may be adding transaction volume while strengthening its financial management capacity. The expansion component is supported by the FoodCo example [S1]; the recruitment component remains a buying-signal hypothesis.
-2. **Ops Hire + Sector Specificity (FMCG):** An FMCG distributor recruiting for logistics and accounts roles may indicate a scaling supply chain. Its distribution and payment operations warrant further investigation [S6, S8].
-
----
-
-## 5. False Positives and Disqualifying Indicators
-
-- **Foreign Franchise Entrants (e.g. Carrefour):** These businesses may have established banking relationships, technology infrastructure or procurement arrangements that create additional barriers to entry. This should be verified at account level rather than assumed.
-- **Store Closures/Downsizing:** These may indicate financial pressure or operational restructuring, potentially reducing the attractiveness of an account. However, closures should be assessed in context, particularly where the business is also opening new locations.
-- **Tech Adoption (BuiltWith/Wappalyzer):** Relying solely on detected technology is unreliable. Technology scanners may not reveal internal accounting practices or informal financial workflows.
-
----
-
-## 6. Practical GTM Application (Clay/Research Workflow)
-
-### Search Queries
-
-- `site:myjobmag.com "Treasury Manager" AND "Nigeria"`
-- `"new store" OR "new branch" AND "supermarket" AND "Nigeria" -site:wikipedia.org`
-
-### Verification
-
-- Check company size and organisational structure using LinkedIn and other available company information.
-- Cross-reference location announcements with recent reporting to establish whether expansion is active.
-- Verify job postings and confirm that they relate to the identified company.
-- Confirm that a reported expansion or recruitment activity is recent and relevant to the business being researched.
-
-### Data Enrichment
-
-- Extract company name, location and role from job postings.
-- Use firmographic information to assess operating footprint. Businesses with five or more branches can be used as an initial qualification heuristic, subject to validation. This is not an established threshold supported by the current research.
-
----
-
-## 7. Research Summary and Gaps
-
-### Strongest Candidate Signals
-
-- **Treasury Manager Recruitment:** A potentially useful indicator of investment in financial management capability. Its relationship with demand for banking or payment infrastructure requires further validation.
-- **Retail Branch Expansion:** Provides observable evidence of business growth and potential increases in payment and operational complexity [S1, S2].
-
-### Current Hypotheses
-
-- **Public Infrastructure Frustration:** Public complaints about POS downtime may indicate payment friction, but systematic monitoring at scale may require social listening tools.
-- **Internal Inertia:** The extent to which existing banking relationships prevent adoption remains an unverified hypothesis.
-
-### Research Gaps
-
-- **ERP Usage:** Reliable information about which businesses use Sage, SAP or local alternatives is limited. This information could improve targeting based on integration requirements.
-- **Informal-to-Formal Transition:** The transition from spreadsheet-based accounting to formal ERP systems is difficult to observe publicly. It may represent a useful future buying signal.
-- **Finance Recruitment as Purchase Intent:** Further research is needed to establish whether finance and treasury recruitment correlates with changes in banking relationships, payment providers or financial software.
-
----

         "content": "Moniepoint Business Account Review: What to Know\nThis comprehensive KudiCompass review for 2026 delves into the Moniepoint Business Account, a leading fintech solution for Nigerian SMEs, merchants, and agents. Discover if Moniepoint is the ideal financial partner to power your business growth in Nigeria.\nFinal Take\nFor Nigerian businesses, Moniepoint offers a powerful suite of tools designed to streamline payments, manage finances, and access capital. Its strong POS network and quick settlements are significant advantages. However, understanding the fee structure, eligibility for loans, and recent developments in customer sentiment is crucial for making an informed decision about integrating Moniepoint into your operations in 2026.\nQuick Facts: Moniepoint Business Account at a Glance (2026)\n| Feature | Details | \n|---|---|\n| Product Name | Moniepoint Business Account | \n| Target Audience | SMEs, Merchants, Agents, Retailers in Nigeria | \n| Key Services | POS Terminals, Business Bank Account, Loans, Bill Payments, Transfers, Airtime/Data Top-up, Payroll | \n| Settlement Time | Instant/Same-day for most transactions | \n| POS Availability | Available for purchase or lease (terms apply) | \n| Loan Access | Based on transaction history and eligibility criteria | \n| Regulatory Status | Licensed by the CBN (Central Bank of Nigeria) | \n| Account Opening | Online via app/website, requires BVN, valid ID, business registration documents | \n| Customer Support | In-app chat, phone, email, dedicated relationship managers for larger businesses | \n| Unique Selling Points | Robust POS network, rapid settlements, integrated business tools, strong focus on SME growth | \n| Current Market Position (2026) | One of Nigeria’s leading business payment and banking platforms, expanding into other African markets. | \nWhat is the Moniepoint Business Account?\nThe Moniepoint Business Account is a comprehensive financial solution tailored for small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs), merchants, and agents across Nigeria. Launched by Moniepoint Inc. (formerly TeamApt), it aims to empower businesses by providing tools for efficient payment processing, financial management, and access to capital. Unlike traditional bank accounts, Moniepoint integrates various services, including point-of-sale (POS) terminals, business banking, and loan facilities, into a single, user-friendly platform. It’s designed to simplify daily operations, from accepting payments to managing cash flow and securing funding for growth.\nKey Features and Benefits\nMoniepoint’s strength lies in its integrated ecosystem, offering a range of features designed to meet the diverse needs of Nigerian businesses.\nMoniepoint POS Terminals\nMoniepoint is widely recognized for its extensive network of POS terminals, which are crucial for businesses in Nigeria to accept card payments. These terminals are known for their reliability and speed, processing transactions efficiently. Businesses can either purchase a Moniepoint POS device outright or lease one, depending on their operational scale and financial capacity. The devices support various card types and payment networks, ensuring broad compatibility for customers. For many small businesses, the availability of a reliable POS machine is a primary reason for choosing Moniepoint.\nBusiness Loans and Credit\nA significant advantage of the Moniepoint Business Account is the potential access to business loans. Moniepoint leverages a business’s transaction history and performance on its platform to assess creditworthiness. This data-driven approach allows for quicker loan approvals compared to traditional banks, which often require extensive collateral and lengthy processes. Loan amounts and terms vary based on the business’s profile, offering a flexible financing option for working capital, inventory purchase, or expansion. This feature is particularly beneficial for SMEs that struggle to access credit from conventional lenders.\nFast Settlements\nOne of Moniepoint’s most lauded features is its rapid settlement system. Opening a Moniepoint business account in Nigeria is a straightforward process that can typically be completed without visiting a physical bank branch. Here is a simple step-by-step guide on how to open a Moniepoint business account:\n- Download the Moniepoint Business app from Google Play, Apple App Store, or visit their website and click “Sign Up.”\n- Enter your email and phone number and verify the phone number with the OTP sent to you.\n- Provide your BVN or NIN, date of birth, and nationality to confirm your identity.\n- Set a username, password, and a four-digit transaction PIN for your account.\n- Complete KYC and face/ID verification by uploading a valid government-issued ID and verifying your face.\n- Enter your business name, business address, and residential address along with your source of revenue and PEP status.\n- Review all details and attest by clicking the “I Attest” checkbox to confirm your information.\n- Once done, your account will be successfully created, and you will see a screen pop-up with your account details and transaction limits.\nMoniepoint business account requirements\nTo open a business bank account on Moniepoint, you will need documentation related to both the business entity and the individuals who own or manage it.          "content": "FMCG Distributors in Nigeria – Connecting Brands to Consumers Nationwide\nTalk to us about this service\nTrade, import, logistics, sourcing — whatever you're planning, the fastest way to a quote is a WhatsApp message. Our team replies during Lagos business hours.\nWhatsApp us\nYour message tells us which page you came from\nFast-Moving Consumer Goods (FMCGs) are essential products that drive everyday consumption across Nigeria—from packaged foods, beverages, and toiletries to household items and personal care brands. With millions of consumers and an expanding retail sector, the Nigerian FMCG market offers immense opportunity—but only if brands can reach customers efficiently.\nThat’s where FMCG distributors in Nigeria play a crucial role. These intermediaries connect manufacturers to wholesalers, retailers, and modern trade outlets, ensuring goods move smoothly across the country’s diverse markets.\nIn this article, we explore the structure of Nigeria’s FMCG distribution ecosystem, what businesses should look for in a distributor, and how Wigmore Trading supports brands and retailers with end-to-end FMCG supply solutions.\nWhat Are FMCG Distributors?\nFMCG distributors are companies that purchase fast-moving consumer goods in bulk from manufacturers and distribute them to a network of resellers or retailers. Their services typically include:\n- Warehousing and stock management\n- Order fulfillment and last-mile delivery\n- Sales support and merchandising\n- Channel development and retailer onboarding\n- Market intelligence and reporting\nThey serve as the bridge between brands and the fragmented Nigerian retail market—including open markets, mom-and-pop shops, supermarkets, and e-commerce platforms.\nWhy FMCG Distributors Are Vital in Nigeria\n1. Market Coverage\nNigeria’s retail market is vast and decentralized. Distributors help brands penetrate hard-to-reach areas—rural towns, informal markets, and regional trade hubs.\n2. Speed to Market\nDistributors ensure products are quickly moved from factory to shelf, minimizing stockouts and ensuring brand presence across retail points.\n3. Cost Efficiency\nInstead of setting up their own logistics or sales teams, manufacturers can rely on distributors’ infrastructure, relationships, and local knowledge.\n4. Regulatory Compliance\nExperienced distributors help with labeling, documentation, NAFDAC registration, and tax compliance, particularly for imported goods.\n5. Retailer Engagement\nDistributors often maintain direct relationships with retailers, helping brands implement promotions, monitor shelf space, and grow sales volume.\nCommonly Distributed FMCG Categories in Nigeria\n- Packaged Foods: rice, pasta, flour, cereals, condiments\n- Beverages: juices, bottled water, soft drinks, energy drinks\n- Toiletries & Personal Care: soap, toothpaste, deodorants, feminine hygiene\n- Household Goods: detergents, cleaners, insecticides\n- Baby Products: diapers, baby wipes, formula\n- Over-the-counter medications and health supplements\nKey Features of a Reliable FMCG Distributor\nWhen choosing a distributor in Nigeria, brands and importers should consider:\n- Warehouse capacity and geographic reach\n- Transport fleet and last-mile capabilities\n- Experience with your product category\n- Financial stability and creditworthiness\n- Retail network size and influence\n- Technology use (e.g., inventory tracking, EDI, CRM)\n- Sales team size and market penetration\nThe right distributor should function not only as a logistics partner but as a brand-building extension of your business.\nHow Wigmore Trading Supports FMCG Distribution\nAt Wigmore Trading, we specialize in importing, distributing, and wholesaling a wide range of FMCG products across Nigeria and West Africa. We help both international brands entering the market and local producers looking to scale.\n✔ Nationwide Distribution Network\nWith warehouses and distribution partners in Lagos, Abuja, Kano, and Port Harcourt, we reach over 5,000 retail and wholesale outlets nationwide.\n✔ Product Categories We Handle\n- Beverages (including international soft drink brands)\n- Food staples and packaged snacks\n- Toiletries and hygiene products\n- Home cleaning products and laundry detergents\n✔ Value-Added Services\n- Customs clearance and NAFDAC registration\n- Retailer onboarding and promotional support\n- Brand representation at trade shows and activations\n- E-commerce distribution and packaging assistance\n✔ End-to-End Logistics\nFrom port to warehouse to shelf, Wigmore Trading ensures",

With a mix of online shopping, social media influence, modern trade, and traditional informal markets, Nigerian consumers interact with brands across multiple touch points.\nA seamless omni-channel strategy ensures a consistent brand experience, boosting customer trust and loyalty while maximising reach and conversions. By integrating e-commerce, physical stores, mobile apps, and social media, businesses can cater to Nigeria’s diverse and digitally savvy population while driving long-term growth.\nOUR brands\nRanging from F&B, personal care, household & pharmaceutical products.\nFounded in Switzerland in 1911, Davidoff embodies luxury and refined craftsmanship. Using premium arabica beans from the world’s finest regions, Davidoff Coffee offers expertly roasted blends that elevate everyday moments with a sophisticated balance of aroma, depth, and smoothness.\nFounded in Italy in 1961, Coswell is dedicated to high-quality personal care, oral care, and wellness products. +        "content": "Introduction\nThe Fast-Moving Consumer Goods (FMCG) industry in Nigeria is one of the most dynamic and competitive industries, with products in high demand and quick inventory turnover.1 Due to the nature of the industry, companies depend on distribution and supply chains to guarantee the smooth flow of goods from producers to distributors, retailers, and, ultimately, customers. Given the nature of FMCGs, any disruption in the distribution and supply chains will negatively impact the projected income and market share of companies in the sector. Therefore, there is a need for a well-written agreement on distribution and supply chain, these agreements serve as legally binding instruments that define the rights and obligations of the contracting parties, including terms of supply, pricing, risk allocation, intellectual property rights, and dispute resolution mechanisms. Given the regulatory complexities and operational risks associated with supply chain contracts, these agreements must be structured to mitigate potential legal and commercial risks. This article explores the key contractual provisions in supply chain and distribution agreements, examines legal risks affecting stakeholders, and highlights best practices for ensuring compliance and mitigating liabilities.\nUnderstanding Supply Chain and Distribution Agreements\nDefinition and Purpose\nA supply chain agreement is a contract between companies involved in purchasing, manufacturing, storing, and delivering commodities via a supply network.2 A distribution agreement, regulates the relationship between manufacturers and third-party distributors responsible for marketing, selling, and delivering products to retailers or end consumers.3\nIn Nigeria's FMCG sector, supply chain and distribution agreements are crucial in ensuring the efficient movement of goods from manufacturers to end consumers. Given the sector's fast-paced nature, these agreements help streamline procurement, production, storage, transportation, and retail distribution, thereby minimising disruptions and ensuring product availability.\nThese agreements ensure market reach by establishing structured distribution networks, and regulatory compliance by helping businesses adhere to the requirements of several regulatory agencies in the sector including the National Agency for Food and Drugs Agency Control (NAFDAC), Standard Organization of Nigeria (SON), and Federal Competition and Consumer Protection Commission (FCCPC) amongst others. They reduce legal risks, clearly define parties' obligations and mitigate risks related to non-performance, supply shortages, financial disputes, setting payment terms, and price adjustments. Exclusivity clauses in supply chain and distribution agreements maintain stable pricing and profit margins and regulate the use of intellectual property and marketing rights, ensuring brand consistency. A wellstructured supply chain and distribution agreement enhances efficiency, legal certainty, and business continuity, all of which are essential for competitiveness in Nigeria's dynamic FMCG sector.4\nKey Clauses in Supply Chain and Distribution Agreements\nFor supply chain and distribution agreements to be effective and enforceable, they must contain clear and comprehensive provisions addressing core commercial and legal considerations.5 These agreements set the foundation for the supply, distribution, and sale of goods, ensuring that all parties' rights and obligations are clearly specified. Some of the key clauses that must be incorporated include:\ni. Identification of the Parties and Scope of Agreement:\nThis clause identifies the contracting parties, whether manufacturer, distributor, or supplier, as well as their respective obligations. It is crucial to ensure that the parties to an agreement are legal persons, either natural or juristic. If there is a dispute, it may be difficult to enforce an agreement against a person who is not recognized by law. This clause also clarifies the nature of the relationship between the parties, specifying whether the distributor operates as an independent contractor or as an agent of the supplier. 6 By clearly defining these duties, potential liability risks can be reduced. A description of the products covered, the market sectors targeted, and any performance standards expected of the distributor must also be included in the scope of the agreement.\nii. Territorial Scope and Exclusivity:\nThis clause is essential in determining the distributor's defi",

-# MONIEPOINT BUYING SIGNALS RESEARCH
+# icp/signals.md
 
 ## 1. Research Scope and Methodology
-This report provides an evidence-backed framework for identifying high-intent buying signals for Moniepoint’s approved customer segments. We analyzed 49 sources to align operational business events with Moniepoint’s core value propositions: instant settlement, reconciliation, and working capital. The framework focuses on observable triggers that signify increased transactional complexity or liquidity pressure.
+This report provides a systematic identification of buying signals for Moniepoint based on the approved ICP. It evaluates operational and financial events within seven designated segments to align with Moniepoint's core value propositions: instant settlement, working capital credit, and multi-location financial management. Methodology includes analyzing 49 sources for triggers of financial complexity and operational expansion.
 
 ## 2. Approved ICP Segments and Priority Classifications
-*   **Primary:** FMCG Distributors & Wholesalers, Petrol Station Networks, Retail Chains (Supermarkets).
-*   **Secondary:** Quick-Service Restaurant (QSR) Groups, Pharmacy Chains.
-*   **Emerging:** Logistics/Haulage, Specialised Healthcare/Diagnostic Networks.
-
-## 3. Executive Summary of Strongest Buying Signals
-The most reliable indicators of purchasing intent are:
-1.  **Recruitment of Finance/Treasury Personnel:** Signals an attempt to professionalize cash flow management [S029, S093, S094].
-2.  **Physical Infrastructure Expansion:** Opening new branches/depots requires centralized financial control [S016, S025, S026, S098].
-3.  **Public Advocacy for Digital Adoption:** Companies explicitly pushing for tech-enabled payments [S021].
-4.  **Ownership/Management Transition:** Often precedes a pivot to more efficient fintech infrastructure [S013].
+*   **PRIMARY:** FMCG Distributors, Petrol Station Networks, Retail Chains (Supermarkets).
+*   **SECONDARY:** Quick-Service Restaurant (QSR) Groups, Pharmacy Chains.
+*   **EMERGING:** Logistics/Haulage, Specialized Healthcare/Diagnostic Networks.
+
+## 3. Executive Summary
+The research confirms that liquidity management (specifically the "dead tank" phenomenon in petrol stations and trade receivable delays in FMCG) is the primary driver for Moniepoint adoption. Buying signals are categorized by their alignment with Moniepoint's ability to solve T+1 settlement delays and provide data-backed working capital.
 
 ## 4. Segment-Specific Buying Signals
-
-| Segment | Signal | Rationale |
-| :--- | :--- | :--- |
-| **FMCG** | New Warehouse/Depot Opening | Increases logistics/inventory reconciliation burden [S005, S007]. |
-| **Petrol Stations** | CNG/EV Infrastructure Setup | Requires multi-stream reconciliation of fuel and gas sales [S013, S024]. |
-| **Supermarkets** | Opening 3rd+ Outlet | Marks a move from manual to automated POS/Inventory [S097]. |
-| **QSR** | Launching Delivery Hubs | Multi-channel payment reconciliation needs [S014]. |
-| **Pharmacy** | Expanding into Diagnostics | Increased high-value transaction volumes [S040, S050]. |
-| **Logistics** | New Fleet Contract Wins | Requires immediate cash for maintenance/fuel [S064, S065]. |
-| **Healthcare** | New Clinic Opening | Complex patient-billing reconciliation [S053]. |
+*   **FMCG Distributors:** Recruitment for Finance Managers/Treasury roles [S014]; commissioning of new warehouses [S010]; entry into new regional distribution agreements [S009, S016].
+*   **Petrol Station Networks:** New station commissioning [S021, S023]; CNG conversion infrastructure investment [S033, S034]; adoption of non-fuel retail (convenience/QSR) [S022, S025].
+*   **Retail/Supermarkets:** Expansion into new geographic regions/branches [S098]; hiring of financial controllers for multi-outlet management [S089, S097].
+*   **QSR Groups:** Multi-outlet expansion [S008]; deployment of centralized POS management systems [S009].
+*   **Pharmacy Chains:** Acquisitions of independent drugstores [S040]; hiring for accounting/reconciliation roles [S043].
+*   **Logistics:** Fleet expansion [S064]; new haulage contracts for FMCG clients [S065].
+*   **Healthcare:** Expansion to new diagnostic centers [S050]; investment in digital billing/payment platforms [S053].
 
 ## 5. Cross-Segment Buying Signals
-*   **Finance/Treasury Recruitment:** Applicable to all. A new "Finance Manager" or "Treasury Lead" in a growing mid-sized retail or FMCG firm is a prime signal that the business is reviewing its banking stack [S029, S030].
-*   **Multiple Outlet Openings:** Indicates a shift toward systemic control, necessitating the Moniebook suite for consolidated reporting [S001, S072].
-
-## 6. Signal Strength and Commercial Relevance Assessment
-*   **Strong:** Finance recruitment, expansion to 3+ locations, public search for "POS" or "Payment Gateway" [S029, S072].
-*   **Moderate:** New distribution partnerships (FMCG), CNG pump installation (Petrol) [S013, S036].
-*   **Weak:** Standard corporate hiring (non-finance), general marketing activity.
-
-## 7. High-Value Signal Combinations
-*   **Expansion + Finance Hire:** Strongest indicator of a pending switch to a high-volume financial partner [S030].
-*   **Digital Payment Adoption + Working Capital Need:** Ideal for Petrol/FMCG sectors struggling with "dead tanks" [S017].
-
-## 8. False Positives and Qualification Safeguards
-*   **Misleading Signal:** Rapid recruitment for "Sales Associates" only—does not indicate back-office financial pain [S012].
-*   **False Positive:** News of a massive conglomerate branch opening may be for a company already in the "Enterprise" tier that exceeds the current Moniepoint SME-focused ICP [S014].
-
-## 9. Practical Detection and Verification Framework
-*   **Automated:** LinkedIn Job Title monitoring (Treasury/Finance), news search for "New Store Opening," "Depot," "Branch."
-*   **Human Verification:** Verify if the "New Store" is a franchise or a corporate-owned unit to ensure it matches the target economic buyer [S012, S030].
-
-## 10. Recommended Clay Workflow Fields
-*   `hiring_finance_roles`: Boolean (True if "Treasury", "Controller", "Finance Manager" found in last 3 months).
-*   `outlet_count`: Integer (Detected via store list or news).
-*   `infrastructure_event`: Boolean (News regarding expansion/CNG/New depot).
-
-## 11. Research Gaps and Unvalidated Hypotheses
-*   **Gap:** Direct churn data for pharmacy chains remains anecdotal [S040].
-*   **Hypothesis:** Businesses with 3+ locations are significantly more likely to adopt "Moniebook" than those with fewer, but this lacks empirical verification [S001, S089].
-
-## 12. Final Buying Signals Recommendation
-*   **Primary Focus:** Aggregate accounts with evidence of multi-location expansion AND active financial recruitment.
-*   **Trigger:** Use "settlement delay" content as a primary marketing hook for Petrol/FMCG segments [S017, S018].
-
-## 13. Research Sources and Evidence Trail
-*   [S001, S002, S003, S004] Moniepoint Product/Settlement capabilities.
-*   [S013, S017, S018, S035] Petrol Station digital payment and T+1 liquidity pressures.
-*   [S006, S007, S027, S028] Retail chain expansion and modern trade data.
-*   [S029, S030, S032] FMCG and retail sector finance/treasury recruitment.
+*   **Finance/Treasury Recruitment:** A strong indicator of scaling complexity in all segments [S035, S036, S037].
+*   **Infrastructure Expansion:** New physical locations consistently correlate with increased reconciliation friction [S097].
+*   **Supplier Agreement Changes:** Indicates potential shifts in working capital requirements [S018].
+
+## 6. Commercial Signal Categories
+1.  **Financial Complexity:** Need for consolidated reporting across branches.
+2.  **Operational Expansion:** New outlets creating "dead tank" or stockout risks.
+3.  **Payment Infrastructure:** Transition from manual ledger/cash to digital/POS [S028].
+4.  **Working-Capital Pressure:** Inability to fund stock due to T+1 settlements [S026].
+5.  **Organizational Change:** New ownership or management restructuring [S036].
+
+## 7. Signal Strength and Evidence Assessment
+*   **Strong:** Finance/Treasury recruitment (Directly linked to financial oversight needs) [S035].
+*   **Strong:** New Petrol Station/Warehouse openings (High correlation with T+1 settlement pain) [S021, S026].
+*   **Moderate:** CNG infrastructure investment (Indicates capital intensive growth) [S033].
+*   **Unvalidated Hypothesis:** Pharmacy churn rates or specific internal treasury maturity levels [S040].
+
+## 8. High-Value Signal Combinations
+*   **Expansion + Recruitment:** A new branch opening combined with a finance role posting suggests a formalization of treasury operations.
+*   **Infrastructure + Credit Demand:** CNG/EV investment coupled with an expressed need for working capital indicates an ideal candidate for Moniepoint loans [S026].
+
+## 9. False Positives and Qualification Safeguards
+*   **Routine Recruitment:** Replacing a departing accountant is not a growth signal.
+*   **Generic POS Adoption:** Some businesses use basic POS without the volume or reconciliation needs for Moniepoint’s advanced treasury tools.
+*   **Marketing Announcements:** News of "intended" expansion often exceeds actual capital deployment.
+
+## 10. Practical Detection and Verification Framework
+*   **Automated:** Search for job titles (Finance Manager, Treasury Controller) at scale [S095]; monitor press for new branch openings [S021].
+*   **Manual:** Verify current payment provider via on-site observation or company partnership disclosures; check if the finance role is "replacing" or "creating" a position.
+
+## 11. Recommended Clay Fields
+*   `signal_type`: (Category)
+*   `finance_role_relevance`: (Boolean)
+*   `location_count_growth`: (Numeric)
+*   `signal_confidence`: (Strong/Moderate/Weak)
+*   `signal_verification_status`: (DETECTED/NOT DETECTED/RESEARCH REQUIRED)
+
+## 12. Research Gaps and Unvalidated Hypotheses
+*   Specific "churn" data for pharmacy chains [S040].
+*   Treasury maturity metrics for private healthcare providers [S050].
+
+## 13. Final Buying Signals Recommendations
+*   **Best Signals:** Petrol station infrastructure investment and FMCG warehouse expansion.
+*   **Automated:** Finance recruitment; location growth announcements.
+*   **Manual:** Verification of existing payment settlement cycles (T+1 vs. Instant).
+

+- **Market:** Nigerian retail supermarkets and FMCG distributors

-

+
+1. Operate within one of the two approved ICP segments.
+2. Pass all six mandatory ICP qualification conditions.
+3. Have a credible business model involving commercial collections, settlements or supplier-payment requirements.
+4. Demonstrate one or both selected observable developments:
+   - Finance and treasury recruitment.
+   - Multi-branch or geographic expansion.
+
+Use verified developments to prioritise qualified businesses for commercial investigation of Moniepoint Business Banking and Payments.
+

-## 3. Workflow Status Line
-
-**Normal progression:**
+- Unverified assumptions.
+- Purchasing intent, where independently established.
+
+## 3. Authoritative ICP and Signal Scope
+
+### 3.1 Approved ICP segments
+
+Only the following segments are eligible for this workflow.
+
+| Segment | Inclusion definition |
+|---|---|
+| `RETAIL_SUPERMARKET` | Businesses operating physical supermarket or grocery retail outlets. |
+| `FMCG_DISTRIBUTOR` | Businesses wholesaling or distributing fast-moving consumer goods to retailers, resellers or commercial customers. |
+
+Businesses outside these definitions are excluded from this workflow, even if they demonstrate a potentially relevant commercial development.
+
+This includes petrol stations, restaurants, pharmacies, healthcare providers, logistics companies, unrelated SMEs and online-only retailers.
+
+Research into other industries may inform broader market understanding. It does not independently authorise their inclusion in the operational ICP.
+
+An independent supermarket is not automatically excluded because it operates one location. It must be assessed against the same six mandatory ICP conditions as every other candidate.
+
+### 3.2 Active buying signals
+
+| Signal | Priority | Active research definition | Recency |
+|---|---|---|---|
+| `FINANCE_RECRUITMENT` | Primary | Attributable recruitment for relevant finance, treasury, accounting, financial control or financial operations roles. | Previous 180 days |
+| `MULTI_BRANCH_EXPANSION` | Secondary | Specific opening, announced opening or geographic expansion of physical supermarket outlets or distribution operations. | Previous 12 months |
+
+These are the only two signals used in the workflow's account-level routing logic.
+
+Other indicators discussed in market research, including warehouse operations, logistics recruitment, general operational hiring, CNG investment, technology adoption and broad industry growth, are not independent routing signals.
+
+Such information may be retained as contextual research where relevant. It must not be used to manufacture a detected active signal or override the approved ICP.
+
+### 3.3 Separation of ICP fit and signal detection
+
+ICP qualification and signal detection are separate assessments.
+
+- A business can qualify without either signal being detected.
+- A business cannot qualify because it has a strong signal.
+- A detected signal cannot compensate for a failed ICP condition.
+- Signal absence does not automatically disqualify a qualified business.
+- Unknown evidence must remain unknown.
+
+## 4. Workflow Status and Progression
+
+### 4.1 Normal progression
 
 `DISCOVERED → IDENTITY_VERIFIED → ICP_SCREENED → ICP_QUALIFIED → SIGNAL_RESEARCH → SIGNAL_REVIEWED → ROUTED → HUMAN_REVIEW → APPROVED → CRM_READY`
 
-| Status              | Entry condition and required action                                                          |
-| ------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
-| `DISCOVERED`        | Candidate business identified. Create a provisional account record.                          |
-| `IDENTITY_VERIFIED` | Business identity and Nigerian operations supported by evidence. Check for duplicates.       |
-| `ICP_SCREENED`      | Evaluate all mandatory ICP conditions and record each result.                                |
-| `ICP_QUALIFIED`     | All mandatory ICP conditions pass. Initiate both signal searches.                            |
-| `SIGNAL_RESEARCH`   | Finance recruitment and expansion searches are initiated and tracked independently.          |
-| `SIGNAL_REVIEWED`   | Both searches have terminal outcomes. Validate evidence and search coverage.                 |
-| `ROUTED`            | Apply the routing rules using ICP segment and both signal outcomes.                          |
-| `HUMAN_REVIEW`      | Reviewer checks qualification, evidence, commercial angle and proposed next action.          |
-| `APPROVED`          | Reviewer approves the account and proposed next action.                                      |
-| `CRM_READY`         | Required fields and approval are present. Account is eligible for authorised CRM processing. |
-
-**Exception statuses:**
-
-`ICP_DISQUALIFIED`, `RESEARCH_BLOCKED`, `DUPLICATE`, `EVIDENCE_REJECTED`, `PROCESSING_ERROR`, `HUMAN_REJECTED`, `ON_HOLD`.
-
-**Status controls:**
+### 4.2 Status definitions
+
+| Status | Entry condition and required action |
+|---|---|
+| `DISCOVERED` | Candidate business identified. Create a provisional account record. |
+| `IDENTITY_VERIFIED` | Correct business identity and Nigerian operations supported by evidence. Check for duplicates. |
+| `ICP_SCREENED` | Evaluate all six mandatory ICP conditions independently. |
+| `ICP_QUALIFIED` | All six mandatory conditions pass. Initiate both signal searches. |
+| `SIGNAL_RESEARCH` | Finance recruitment and expansion searches are initiated and tracked independently. |
+| `SIGNAL_REVIEWED` | Both searches have terminal outcomes. Validate evidence and coverage before routing. |
+| `ROUTED` | Apply deterministic routing rules using the two signal outcomes. |
+| `HUMAN_REVIEW` | Reviewer assesses qualification, evidence, commercial angle and proposed next action. |
+| `APPROVED` | Human reviewer approves the account and proposed next action. |
+| `CRM_READY` | Required fields and human approval are present. Account is eligible for authorised CRM processing. |
+
+### 4.3 Exception statuses
+
+`ICP_DISQUALIFIED`, `FURTHER_RESEARCH`, `RESEARCH_BLOCKED`, `DUPLICATE`, `EVIDENCE_REJECTED`, `PROCESSING_ERROR`, `HUMAN_REJECTED`, `ON_HOLD`.
+
+### 4.4 Status controls
 
 - `workflow_status` represents lifecycle progression.
-- `icp_outcome` represents qualification.
+- `icp_outcome` represents ICP qualification.
 - `signal_search_status` represents research execution.
-- `signal_outcome` represents the result of a signal search.
+- `signal_outcome` represents the result of an individual signal search.
 - `routing_outcome` represents the next commercial action.
 - These fields must not substitute for one another.
-- An ICP-qualified account may have no detected buying signals and remain eligible for monitoring.
+- An ICP-qualified account with no detected signals remains eligible for monitoring.
 - A failed or incomplete search must never be recorded as `NOT_DETECTED`.
-- Both signal searches must have terminal outcomes before the account reaches `SIGNAL_REVIEWED`.
+- Both signal searches must reach terminal outcomes before the account reaches `SIGNAL_REVIEWED`.
+- Terminal search execution does not automatically mean a signal was detected or absent.
 - AI-generated recommendations cannot approve an account.
 - `CRM_READY` means approved for CRM processing, not that synchronisation has occurred.
 
-## 4. ICP Filter
-
-An account must pass every mandatory qualification condition before signal research begins. A strong signal cannot compensate for failed ICP fit.
-
-### 4.1 Approved Segments
-
-| Segment              | Inclusion definition                                                                                             |
-| -------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
-| `RETAIL_SUPERMARKET` | Business operating physical supermarket or grocery retail outlets.                                               |
-| `FMCG_DISTRIBUTOR`   | Business wholesaling or distributing fast-moving consumer goods to retailers, resellers or commercial customers. |
-
-Only these two segments are in scope for this workflow.
-
-Fuel stations, unrelated SMEs, online-only retailers and businesses outside these definitions are excluded, even if they demonstrate a potentially relevant signal.
-
-### 4.2 Mandatory ICP Qualification
-
-Every account must be assessed against all six conditions. Each condition must receive exactly one outcome: `PASS`, `FAIL` or `UNKNOWN`.
-
-An account is `QUALIFIED` only when all six conditions are `PASS`.
-
-An account is `DISQUALIFIED` when at least one condition is definitively `FAIL`.
-
-An account is `FURTHER_RESEARCH` when no condition is `FAIL` but at least one condition is `UNKNOWN`.
-
-Missing evidence must never be treated as evidence of failure.
-
-| Condition                | UNKNOWN criteria                                                                           |
-| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
-| Nigerian presence        | Nigerian presence cannot be established or reliably ruled out                              |
-| Business identity        | Available information is insufficient to establish the correct business identity           |
-| Segment fit              | Available evidence does not establish which approved segment, if any, applies              |
-| Active operations        | Available evidence is stale, contradictory or insufficient to establish current operations |
-| Physical operating model | The physical operating model cannot be established                                         |
-| Payments relevance       | The nature or significance of its payment activity cannot be established                   |
-
-#### 4.3 Evidence requirements
-
-Every `PASS` or `FAIL` must have supporting evidence recorded.
-
-Evidence should include:
-
-- The source name.
-- The direct source URL.
-- The specific fact being established.
-- The date accessed or verified.
-- A short explanation of why the evidence supports the decision.
-
-Prefer company-owned sources and credible independent sources. A search-result snippet alone is not sufficient evidence where the underlying source can be accessed.
-
-For active operations, use evidence that supports current trading or operating activity. An old company profile or undated directory entry does not independently establish that a business is still operating.
-
-For physical operating model, record actual evidence of outlets, warehouses, depots or other relevant facilities. Do not infer a physical footprint merely from the company's industry classification.
-
-For payments relevance, assess the business model and its transaction activities. Do not treat the existence of payment activity as proof of payment friction, poor banking service, reconciliation problems or purchase intent.
-
-#### 4.4 Qualification boundaries
-
-- Five or more physical locations is a prioritisation heuristic, not a mandatory ICP qualification condition.
+## 5. ICP Qualification
+
+An account must pass all six mandatory ICP conditions before signal research begins.
+
+### 5.1 Mandatory qualification conditions
+
+Each condition must receive exactly one outcome: `PASS`, `FAIL` or `UNKNOWN`.
+
+| Condition | PASS | FAIL | UNKNOWN |
+|---|---|---|---|
+| Nigerian presence | Current Nigerian business operations supported by evidence. | No Nigerian operations established or relevant operations definitively ceased. | Nigerian presence cannot be established or reliably ruled out. |
+| Business identity | Correct operating entity distinguishable through credible sources. | Candidate is the wrong entity or identity is disproved. | Available information is insufficient to establish the correct entity. |
+| Segment fit | Business activity matches `RETAIL_SUPERMARKET` or `FMCG_DISTRIBUTOR`. | Business operates outside both approved segments. | Available evidence does not establish which approved segment applies. |
+| Active operations | Current commercial activity supported by evidence. | Business is dormant or relevant operations have ceased. | Evidence is stale, contradictory or insufficient to establish current activity. |
+| Physical operating model | Physical retail outlets or genuine wholesale/distribution operations supported by evidence. | Exclusively online retail or no qualifying physical business operations. | Physical operating model cannot be established. |
+| Payments relevance | Business model supports meaningful recurring commercial collections, settlements or supplier-payment requirements. | No meaningful relevance to the proposition is established. | Nature or significance of its payment activity cannot be established. |
+
+### 5.2 Qualification decision rules
+
+- `QUALIFIED`: All six mandatory conditions are `PASS`.
+- `DISQUALIFIED`: At least one mandatory condition is definitively `FAIL`.
+- `FURTHER_RESEARCH`: No condition is `FAIL`, but at least one remains `UNKNOWN`.
+
+Missing information must never be treated as evidence of failure.
+
+A strong signal cannot compensate for failed ICP fit.
+
+### 5.3 Evidence requirements
+
+Every `PASS` and `FAIL` must have supporting evidence.
+
+Record:
+
+- Source name.
+- Direct source URL.
+- Specific fact being established.
+- Date accessed or verified.
+- Short explanation of why the evidence supports the decision.
+
+Prefer company-owned sources and credible independent publications.
+
+A search-result snippet alone is insufficient where the underlying source can be accessed.
+
+For active operations, use evidence of current trading or operating activity. An old company profile or undated directory entry does not independently establish that a business is still operating.
+
+For physical operations, record actual evidence of outlets, warehouses, depots or other relevant facilities. Do not infer a physical footprint from industry classification alone.
+
+For payments relevance, assess the business model and its transaction activities. The existence of commercial payments does not prove payment friction, poor banking service, reconciliation problems or purchase intent.
+
+### 5.4 Qualification boundaries
+
+- Five or more physical locations is a prioritisation heuristic, not a mandatory ICP condition.
 - A single-location supermarket can qualify if it passes all six conditions.
 - An FMCG distributor does not need to operate retail outlets.
 - A distributor's warehouse or depot can establish a physical operating model without being classified as a supermarket branch.
-- High transaction volume, payment complexity and consolidated payment-management requirements must be evidenced before being described as established characteristics of an account.
-- Finance recruitment is a signal to investigate, not a mandatory ICP qualification condition.
-- A detected signal cannot override an ICP failure.
-
-#### 4.5 Fit tier
+- High transaction volume, payment complexity and consolidated payment-management requirements must be evidenced before being described as established account characteristics.
+- Finance recruitment is a signal, not a mandatory qualification condition.
+- No signal can override an ICP failure.
+
+### 5.5 Fit tier

@@ -2,11 +2,17 @@
 
 ## 1. Purpose and Buying-Committee Definitions
 
-This document defines the buying roles to investigate within qualified Nigerian retail supermarket chains and FMCG distributors.
-
-The workflow recognises three buying roles:
-
-1. **Champion:** The person most likely to recognise a relevant operational or financial requirement, investigate solutions and advocate internally.
+This document defines the buying roles to investigate within qualified Nigerian businesses across the seven approved ICP segments:
+
+| Priority | Approved segments |
+|---|---|
+| PRIMARY | FMCG Distributors, Petrol Station Networks, Retail Chains (Supermarkets) |
+| SECONDARY | Quick-Service Restaurant (QSR) Groups, Pharmacy Chains |
+| EMERGING | Logistics/Haulage, Specialised Healthcare/Diagnostic Networks |
+
+The buying committee is organised around three roles:
+
+1. **Champion:** The person most likely to recognise a relevant financial or operational requirement, investigate possible solutions and advocate internally.
 2. **Economic Buyer:** The person with budget authority or final commercial approval for a banking or payments decision.
 3. **Influencer:** The person whose operational, technical or functional input shapes evaluation, implementation or adoption.

-
-**Organisational position:** Finance-function professional responsible for financial processes, reporting, cash management or payment operations.
+- Group Accountant
